--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). It transitions from Dürer’s own closing remarks to a posthumous note added by his editors or friends, explaining the state of the manuscript at the time of his death.</meta>

Whoever now undertakes and follows this my instruction, first forming a correct <term>proportion</term> <note>the mathematical relationship between the parts of the human body</note> through the given measures, then positioning them orderly and bending them into the <term>ground plan</term> <note>original: "grund legē," referring to the horizontal layout of a figure's footprint or base</note>, then elevating them and bringing them into <term>perspective</term>, and thus further artistically drawing them into the painting or work—he will soon realize what usefulness may result for him. Without doubt, he will find far more than is indicated or reported here, notwithstanding that this my instruction may be considered difficult in some places. Yet it is true that what is difficult to understand will not be learned without effort and diligence. And with that, I wish, gracious Lord <note>likely a reference to King Ferdinand I, to whom the work was dedicated</note>, to make an end to my writing for this time. And if God grants it in His time, I shall write further on what else belongs to painting, so that such art does not rest on <term>mere practice</term> <note>original: "brauch," referring to working by habit or intuition without theoretical knowledge</note> alone, but may also in time be learned and understood from a right and orderly foundation, to the praise of God and for the use and pleasure of all art lovers.

---

Although the pious and ingenious Albrecht Dürer wrote these four books, he yet only reviewed and corrected the first one. Before he could come to the other three, the swiftness of death overtook him, so that he was not able to review them again. It is quite possible, had he had time, he might have changed, added to, or diminished <note>removed or shortened</note> certain things. Nonetheless, his good friends considered it more useful that the other three books also be issued uncorrected <note>unrevised</note> than that they should remain suppressed <note>original: "vndergedruct," meaning kept from being printed</note>. This they did regardless of whether there should be anything in them to be improved (though they do not think so). If God had granted him a longer life, he would have brought to light and given many more wonderful, rare, and artistic things, especially regarding the art of painting <term>landscapes</term>, colors, and the like. He also particularly intended a longer and clearer [treatise on] perspective than he had previously described and published. But God, who has ordained all things for the best, prevented this not without cause; His name be praised and honored eternally.

<summary>This text concludes Albrecht Dürer's seminal work on human proportion, emphasizing the need for artistic theory over mere habit. It also contains a posthumous note by his editors explaining that Dürer died before he could finish revising the final three books of the series.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, perspective, artistic theory, landscape painting, posthumous publication, German Renaissance, geometry in art</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the formal opening of an Imperial Privilege or patent. It establishes the legal protection granted to Agnes Dürer for her late husband's artistic and theoretical works.</meta>

<sig>3</sig>

# **W**e, Charles the Fifth, by God’s grace elected Roman Emperor, at all times
increaser of the Empire <note>original: "merer des Reychs," a translation of the Latin *Semper Augustus*, a traditional title for the Emperor implying he expands or strengthens the realm</note>, etc. In Germany, Spain, both Sicilies, Jerusalem, Hun-
gary, Dalmatia, Croatia, etc., King; Archduke of Austria, Duke of Burgun-
dy, etc.; Count of Habsburg, Flanders, and Tyrol, etc. We acknowledge publicly with this letter
and make known to everyone: Whereas the honorable, our dear and devout Agnes, the sur-
viving widow of the late Albrecht Dürer, has brought before our Imperial government in the Holy
Empire how the aforementioned Albrecht, her husband <note>The term *Hauswirt* literally means "house-master," used here for husband.</note>, brought together several books on human
proportion <term>human proportion: the mathematical study of the parts of the human body and their relationship to each other, a central theme of Renaissance art</term> out of true art and perspective through his own diligence and invention, with no
small effort, and had intended to bring these same books to the press
so that they might be useful and practiced by those who love such art; And however,
the same Dürer died recently and before the publication of such art-books; And now she,
the widow, has had such books partly cut into forms <note>This refers to the woodcut blocks used for printing illustrations.</note> and is willing to print them;
Yet some have dared to take the art discovered by her husband and some of that
human proportion through improper ways, bringing it—along-
side others—under their own title, and as if they, and not Dürer, had discovered it, into print.
In addition, she had to fear (as often happens when something is discovered with such diligence,
effort, and work, and brought into print for the common good, that others
immediately pirate it <note>original: "nachdrucken," meaning to reprint without permission</note> and thus seek and conduct their own profit in an unfair manner, to the
disadvantage of the inventor) that if she were to let her deceased husband’s discovered art
be printed, others—to her significant disadvantage—would immediately pirate them
if this were not prevented. And she has humbly called upon and asked us to
appear with gracious provision and help. Therefore, so that the
above-mentioned art and invention of Dürer be not withheld, and also so that the aforementioned widow
is not defrauded or harmed thereby, we give to the same widow this following
freedom <term>freedom: here meaning a "privilege" or early form of copyright protection</term>; We also hereby give her this, by Roman Imperial power, knowingly
and by the strength of this letter: so that no one, whoever they may be, intends to bring the late Albrecht Dürer’s
above-mentioned discovered art and books—or any excerpt of them—into print in his own name,
as if he had made them, or otherwise, without the consent of the said widow,
or let them be published or sold. Furthermore, that no one shall pirate the aforementioned books, art, and proportion
of the named widow, if she has brought them or lets them be brought into print, for ten years
immediately following the publication of the same print, nor sell them so pirated.
And we command thereupon all and every one of our and the Empire’s subjects,
and especially all painters, book-printers, image-printers, or art-printers, carriers, and sellers, and each
one individually, by Roman Imperial power, and under the penalty of ten marks of fine gold <note>A significant monetary fine; "fine gold" refers to pure, high-quality metal.</note>,
half to be paid to us in our Imperial chamber, and the other half to the aforementioned widow
without fail; also under the loss of such prints and pirated copies, we hereby earnestly
will that no one shall claim the late Albrecht Dürer’s discovered art-books,
or their excerpts and supplements, as if he had made them, nor otherwise
bring them into print or let them be published. In addition, the aforementioned books and art, which
the said widow has let or would let be published in print, shall not be pirated in the above-stipulated time
of ten years, nor shall pirated copies be carried to market or sold in any way; as
dear as the avoidance of the aforementioned penalty and the loss of your prints and pirated copies
is to you, all and each of you, which prints and pirated copies the said widow...

<summary>This document is an Imperial Privilege granted by Emperor Charles V to Agnes Dürer, protecting the posthumous publication of Albrecht Dürer’s theoretical works on human proportion from piracy and plagiarism for ten years.</summary>
<keywords>Charles V, Albrecht Dürer, Agnes Dürer, copyright, printing privilege, human proportion, Renaissance art, woodcuts, piracy</keywords>

Now I want to make a long, thin man / and his height shall be ten of his heads long, and I will use the previously described method <note>Dürer refers to his system of using fractions of the total body height to determine proportions.</note> / and first measure the length of the limbs.

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>The transcription mentions heavy ink bleed-through and a "MACKINTOSH" collector's stamp, indicating this leaf was part of a physical collection before being digitized. The content continues the commemorative theme for Albrecht Dürer found in humanist collections of the 16th century.</meta>

# Epitaph for the Same <note>Referring to the artist Albrecht Dürer.</note>

After Albrecht had adorned the world with his painting,
And all things were filled with such charming art,
"Now it remains," he said, "for the high heaven to be painted,"
Soon, leaving the earth, he sought the bright stars.

### Another

Genius, integrity, sincerity, prudence, virtue,
Art, piety, and faith—here they lie entombed.

### Another by the same author <note>These verses were composed by Willibald Pirckheimer (1470–1530), a prominent Nuremberg lawyer and humanist who was Dürer's closest friend.</note>

If life could be returned through tears, O Albrecht,
Your body would not be covered by this humble soil.
But because the Fates are moved neither by tears nor weeping,
We pay our funeral rites only through mourning.

# Sacred to his most blessed memory

To the memory of Albrecht Dürer, a most excellent man and the most perfect painter of his age. He was not only the first among the Germans to enhance and illuminate the art of painting and subject it to a more rigorous law <note>This refers to Dürer’s efforts to move art from a "craft" to a "science" through his published treatises on geometry and human proportion.</note>, but he also began to commend it to posterity through his writings. For this reason—but especially for his refined character, his singular wisdom, and his modesty—he was most beloved by his fellow citizens of Nuremberg, and indeed by all foreigners. He was most highly esteemed by the Divine <note>A formal honorific for deceased rulers.</note> Maximilian and his grandson Charles, the Emperors, as well as by Ferdinand, King of Hungary and Bohemia, who supported him with a generous annual stipend and followed his career with the highest favor. He died, to the great longing of his friends, on the 6th of April <note>original: "viij. idus Aprilis," using the Roman calendar system</note>, in the year 1528, at the age of 57.

->Willibald Pirckheimer<-
->placed this monument for his most upright friend.<- <note>original: "M. P." for "Monumentum Posuit"</note>

The text, authored by Willibald Pirckheimer, celebrates Dürer not just as a master painter but as a scholar and a favorite of the Habsburg Emperors.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Willibald Pirckheimer, Nuremberg, Renaissance art, Emperor Maximilian I, Emperor Charles V, funeral honors, 1528, German humanism</keywords>

Following the man described previously, I will now show a proportional woman <note>Dürer uses the term "weyb," which in 16th-century German simply meant "woman," though today it carries a more archaic or blunt tone.</note> in the same order as mentioned before. First, I will establish the points for the length of the body, and measure within those the length of the limbs.

From the crown of the head to the end of the chin: a 1/10 part.
From the end of the chin upwards to the edge of the forehead: a 1/12 part.
In these twelve parts, I divide the face as described before.
And from the crown of the head to the end of the jaw <note>original: "tröllens," referring to the jawline or the area under the chin.</note> and the chin: an 1/18 part and a 1/19 part.
To the height of the shoulder flesh: an 1/8 part.
To the hollow of the throat: two 1/13 parts.
To the height of the shoulder: a 1/6 part.
From the shoulder height to the height of the breasts: a 1/22 part.
To under the armpits: a 1/16 part.
To the nipples <note>original: "tütlein," a diminutive for breasts or nipples.</note>: an 1/11 part.
To under the breast: a 1/9 part.
To the waist: two 1/11 parts.
From the waist to the navel: a 1/40 part.
To the end of the hip: a 1/10 part.
To the end of the belly: two 1/17 parts.
To the cleft of the woman: a 1/13 part and a 1/14 part.
To the end of the private parts: a 1/12 part and a 1/13 part.
To the end of the buttocks: an 1/11 part and an 1/12 part.
From the end of the buttocks down into the leg: a 1/12 part.
From the end of the sole upward to the middle of the knee, I set: two 1/7 parts.
To the bottom of the ankle bone: a 1/38 part.
From the middle of the knee downward to the foot or instep, I set: a 1/4 part.
To the end of the inner calf: a 1/9 part.
To the end of the outer calf: a 1/10 part.
To under the knee: a 1/30 part.
From the middle of the knee to above the knee: a 1/30 part.
And I make the foot long by: a 1/14 part and a 1/15 part.

Next, I determine the length of the arm:
From the shoulder joint to the elbow: two 1/11 parts.
From the elbow to the tips of the fingers: a 1/4 part.
From the tips of the fingers to the wrist joint: an 1/11 part.

<column-break/>

Now that I have marked the lengths of the limbs with points and their numbers instead of the three upright lines, I will first determine the thickness <note>In this context, "thickness" refers to the width or girth of the body parts as seen from the front or side.</note> of the woman according to the sides.

Through the head at the point of the forehead: a 1/33 part.
Across the eyebrows: an 1/11 part.
Across the nose and head: a 1/12 part.
Across the neck and chin: a 1/14 part.
And the neck below the jaw: a 1/24 part.
At the height of the shoulder flesh: a 1/22 part.
At the hollow of the throat: a 1/17 part.
At the height of the shoulders: a 1/13 part.
Across the breasts: a 1/10 part.

->E I<-

<summary>This page presents a detailed system of anatomical proportions for the female body, utilizing a list of measurements and a posterior woodcut figure. It illustrates the Renaissance effort to standardize human beauty through mathematical ratios and precise observation.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing human proportions, moving from the general measurements of the female figure on the previous page to the mechanical posing of figures and a detailed coordinate system for the male head. It is part of Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion."</meta>

These previously described figures I bend at the joints through the spine <note>original: "ruckgrad"</note> and in all other limbs, as everyone should already know; but so that an understanding of it is shown here, I want to place the spine in the rear fourth of the body's thickness for both the aforementioned figures when seen from the side, and mark it with small triangles. Likewise, for the front-facing figures, indicate some of those same joints with small rings. And although I have measured all the figures here before to the same length from a divided scale for the sake of less effort, yet if one wanted to put them into a work and arrange the figures together, the thinner figures should always be made longer than the thick ones.

And when the man and the woman of one type are arranged together, the line of the woman from which she is measured shall be taken an 18th part shorter than the man's line is; otherwise, the woman would seem larger than the man, because her naked flesh should be drawn fleshier and softer <note>original: "mölleter"</note> than the man's, but the man of a harder constitution <note>original: "geprech"</note> and less fleshy.

This wide difference of the previously made measurements of the figures, from the strongest to the weakest, I have set down for this reason: so that everyone may take cause from it, through which ways he may change and distinguish his undertaking as he pleases, as I will further show in the following little books.

As I have shown four kinds of differences in the figures here before, I will subsequently describe and draw a man more clearly than before, but use the previous order. First, make the head seen from the side in a **square** <term>vierung<gloss>a square or quadrate used as a geometric frame for proportion</gloss></term>, a height of one-eighth of the man's length and just as wide.

But the measure for this head I will take in the drawing from a longer line than that in which the aforementioned figures are described, because of a greater necessity to show the small parts more clearly.

When I set this square, I mark the front upright side of the plan where the nose shall stand out with an **a.** and the rear upright side with **b.** But the upper transverse side with a **c.** and the lower with a **d.** Thereafter I divide the parts in the face; first according to the length with horizontal lines <term>zwerchlinien<gloss>horizontal or cross-lines</gloss></term> all parallel, and set for each its letter; these show the high and low parts of the face.

To the first, I draw a horizontal line **e.** above the side **d.** a 10th part high, as I mentioned before in the man of eight heads' length. This line separates the hair in front and the bareness of the forehead; but the rear crown of the head <note>original: "wirbel"</note> I set a third higher between **c.** and **e.** Thereafter I divide **e.** **d.** with two lines **f.** **g.** into three equal fields. The upper line **f.** shall touch the eyebrows and the top of the ears, but the line **g.** the bottom of the nose, ears, and the head has its indentation at the back in that same place on the neck, as was partly shown before. Thereafter I divide **e.** **f.** with two points into three equal fields, and draw through the lower point with a horizontal line **h.** This line touches above the eyebrows the masculine bulge of the forehead; from there the forehead rounds itself upward to the beginning of the hair. But if I divide **f.** **g.** with two points into three equal fields, I cut off the upper third with a horizontal line **j.** <note>original: "J."</note> so that between **f.** **j.** the whole eye is contained, and the two corners of the eyes end at the back and front in the middle between **f.** **j.** But if I divide **f.** **g.** with three points into four equal fields, I cut off the lowest fourth with a horizontal line **k.** This line touches the top of the nostrils <term>naspellen<gloss>an archaic term for the wings of the nose or nostrils</gloss></term>; also the lower earlobe <term>ozipffellen<gloss>referring to the lobe of the ear</gloss></term> is contained between **k.** **g.** Thereafter I divide **g.** **d.** with a line **l.** into two equal fields; this line touches the top of the chin, as the **d.** does the bottom. But if I divide **g.** **l.** with a line **m.** in two, this line goes through the middle of the mouth. The upper lip I make in the lower third between **g.** **m.** but the upper

<summary>This page details the mechanical posing of proportion figures using geometric markers and explains the aesthetic differences between male and female builds. It introduces a precise alphabetical grid system for mapping the features of the human head within a square frame.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, geometry, facial grid, male vs female proportions, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing the human head, specifically focusing on the geometric construction of the profile (side view). It follows the previous discussion of the general proportions of the male figure, now applying a grid system to map facial features like the nose, lips, and chin.</meta>

Two-thirds I leave for the philtrum <note>original: "rynlein" – the vertical groove between the base of the nose and the upper lip</note> under the nose. I place the lower lip in the upper half-part between **l** and **m**, but in the lower half-part, I place the hollow indentation under the lip above the chin. Thus, with these horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerchlinien"</note>, the most prominent parts of the high and low points of the head are indicated.

Furthermore, I wish to indicate the depth in the face with vertical parallel lines; and by "depth," understand what recedes from the front where the nose stands out.

First, I divide the two vertical sides **a.b.** with six vertical lines **n.o.p.q.r.s** into seven equal fields. The first line **n** touches the front of the eyeball and the corners of the mouth. But I end the back corners of the eyes in the middle between the vertical lines **n** and **o**, for these things should be understood as doubled on both sides in practice. After that, I draw a diagonal line <note>original: "ort lini"</note> from the corner formed by the horizontal side **c** and vertical **n** into the corner of the two lines **a** and **f**; there I place a **t**. After that, I draw another diagonal line, but from the aforementioned corner **c.n.** into the corner formed by the vertical **a** and the horizontal **g**; there I place a **v**.

But the line **n.t.** shall touch the forehead with its form, and above the line **n.v.** I draw the shape <note>original: "possen"</note> of the nose. And where the horizontal line **e** intersects the diagonal line **n.t.**, there I mark the beginning of the hair above the bare forehead. After that, I draw a vertical line midway between the two verticals **a** and **n** and between the horizontals **d** and **g**; this touches the front of the upper lip of the mouth and the front of the chin. After that, I divide **n.r.** between the horizontals **m** and **d** with two vertical lines into three equal fields; the front-most touches the front of the lower lip, but the rear touches the end of the hollow indentation between the lower lip and above the chin. I draw the eyebrows nearly to the line **o**. I also raise them in their middle a little above the horizontal **f**. And between the two verticals **q** and **r** and between the horizontals **f** and **g**, I enclose the entire ear; however, I draw the lower earlobe in the front half-part between **q** and **r** and between the two horizontals **f** and **g**. I place the rear whorl of the hair on the head between the verticals **s** and **b** and horizontals **c** and **e**, at the third-part toward the vertical **s** and also a third-part above the horizontal **e**. On the horizontal **g** between the verticals **s** and **b**, at the nearest quarter-part toward **s**, I set the head upon the neck. After that, I draw the contour lines of the side-view face within and near these intersecting lines, so that the top of the head touches the horizontal side **c** between the verticals **p** and **q**. Likewise, the rear vertical side **b** between the horizontals **h** and **f**, from there down to the neck, and the neck itself all the way down through the square <note>original: "vierung"</note>.

After that, I draw the head down the front to the forehead, and then the forehead, nose, mouth, and chin; the nose touches the vertical side **a** between the horizontals **f** and **g**. Thus, the entire head is enclosed in this square, touching all four sides. But from the chin, I draw the jawbone backward and upward to behind the ear. After that, I draw the eyes and brows, and likewise the ears in their place. I also draw the bareness of the face where the hair begins, and proceed from the front whorl of the forehead over the horizontal line **e**, often between the two verticals **p** and **q**, back down to the front of the mid-ear. After that, I draw the front of the neck under the horizontal **d** downward behind the vertical line **o**, a quarter-part toward the vertical **p**. If I wish, however, I make the neck a 14th part thick, or two 27th parts. Whoever wishes to use these things should pay close attention to the curved contour lines, how they are drawn in and out near the straight lines; for whoever does not take heed of this will quickly fall into deformity <note>original: "vngestalt"</note>, yet the cross-lines in the square never let things run entirely out of proportion.

Now that I have finished the side-view head <note>original: "nebensichtig haupt"</note>, I will proceed to make the front-view <note>original: "fursichtig"</note> in the other square, which will be of equal height with the first, but narrower.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer details a geometric grid system for drawing the human head in profile, using horizontal and vertical coordinates to place features like the philtrum, lips, chin, and ears. He emphasizes that following these cross-lines prevents anatomical deformity and prepares the reader for the subsequent frontal view.</summary>
<keywords>proportions, Albrecht Dürer, human head, profile view, grid system, geometry, facial features, anatomy, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting the human head. Having established the side profile on the previous page, the author now explains how to project those measurements forward to create a perfectly proportional frontal view and the back of the head using a grid system.</meta>

First, I draw all the horizontal parallel lines <note>original: "zwerch parallell linien"</note> of the first square of the side-facing view <note>original: "nebensichtigen angesichts" — refers to the profile</note> forward, out from the square as far as I need them, in the same order as the letters stand from **c** to **d**. These lines in the forward-facing view <note>original: "fursichtigen angesicht" — the frontal view</note> divide all high and low points exactly the same as in the side view; after that, I draw vertical lines through these horizontal lines, which will indicate all the widths of the parts in the forward-facing view.

First, I draw two vertical lines **a** and **b** on both sides through the previously drawn horizontal lines, and I set them a 10th part away from each other; after that, I divide the space between the two sides **a** and **b** into five equal fields with four vertical parallel lines, **c, d, e, f**. In the middle field **d. e.**, I place the crown <note>original: "scheytel"</note> in the center between the horizontal lines **c** and **e**. After that, I place the nose in the middle, so that it touches with its nostrils <note>original: "naspellen"</note> **d** and **e** between the horizontal lines **k** and **g**. I also let the two corners of the mouth touch the vertical lines **d** and **e** on the horizontal line **m**, and the chin remains at the bottom, also between the vertical lines **d** and **e**. After that, I set the two eyes so that they touch the four vertical lines **c, d, e, f** with their four corners, between the horizontal lines **f** and **J**.

After that, I divide the vertical spaces **a. c.** and **f. b.** in the middle from each other with two vertical lines **g** and **h**. These two lines **g** and **h** touch the height of the cheeks on each side at the horizontal line **k**, and the eyebrow ends on both sides in the middle between the vertical lines **g. c.** and **f. h.** under the horizontal line **f**. Furthermore, I divide **a. g.** and **h. b.** in the middle from each other with two vertical lines **i** and **k**. These two vertical lines separate the bare forehead from the hair on both sides between the horizontal lines **e** and **f**, but the head exceeds these two lines on both sides so that half the ears are covered between the horizontal lines **f** and **g**. I also draw the two ears on both sides between **a. i.** and **k. b.** between the horizontal lines **f** and **g**. But above the horizontal line **f**, they exceed the two vertical sides **a** and **b** and approach the two vertical lines **i** and **k** at the bottom with their earlobes <note>original: "zipffellen" — literally 'little tips'</note>.

Now that the vertical lines are drawn through the horizontal lines—which indicate all widths, heights, and lows in the forward-facing view—I then draw the form lines of the face into this division. First, the head from the top, where it touches the horizontal line **c** in the middle between the two vertical lines **d** and **e**; from there, rounding out on both sides to the vertical lines **a** and **b** at the horizontal line **h**; from there down to the ears at the horizontal line **f**, midway between **a. i.** and **k. b.** After that, I draw the bare forehead and the jawbones through the two vertical lines **g** and **h** on the horizontal line **m** all the way down to the chin; and from the aforementioned ends, I draw the neck down from both sides onto the horizontal line **d**, where I make it a 15th part wide, or a little wider. After that, I draw the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin into it, along with the primary features that the face requires.

Once that is finished, I make the third square in there, the back of the head <note>original: "hinderwertige haubt"</note>, according to the outer lines of the forward-facing view, and I set the back crown <note>original: "wirbel" — the whorl or vertex of the head</note> in its place, and draw the rounding of the head onto the neck.

If I wish to lay out this above-described head in a ground plan, I do this through a triangle <note>original: "triangel"</note> which I will show hereafter, so that I may change one given thing into another, yet bring each one forth proportionally <note>original: "gleichförmig"</note>. I designate this triangle with **1, 2, 3**; it should always have a right angle so that it may be used on all sides and however one needs it. It can have equal or unequal sides, long or short. In it, I take whichever side I want for myself and mark it with points; but here I set approximately **4, 5, 6** etc. From these, I draw parallel lines toward one side of the triangle to the third side; thus they divide that same side just as the first is divided. If I then go back from the newly formed points with parallel lines, as before, toward the undivided side, it will also be divided proportionally, just like the first and the second.

<summary>This page explains how to construct a frontal view of a human head by projecting horizontal lines from a profile view onto a vertical grid. It also introduces a geometric method using a right-angled triangle to scale and transfer these proportions accurately.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, frontal view, technical drawing, grid system, facial features, geometry, scaling triangle, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page is the dedication of Albrecht Dürer's "Manual of Measurement" (Underweysung der Messung), first published in 1525. It addresses his friend Willibald Pirckheimer and sets the stage for the technical treatise that follows.</meta>

<sig>A ij</sig> <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early printers and bookbinders to ensure the pages were gathered in the correct order for binding.</note>

->To the Honorable and well-regarded Lord **Willibald Pirckheimer**, <note>Pirckheimer (1470–1530) was a famous German humanist, lawyer, and Dürer's closest friend.</note> Councilor to his Imperial Majesty, etc., my favorable dear Lord and beneficial friend, I, **Albrecht Dürer**, offer my willing service.<-

Although, favorable Lord and friend, I do not doubt that some will undertake to criticize this project of mine—because I, as an unlearned man <note>Dürer often emphasized his lack of a formal university education (which would have required learning Latin) to show that his knowledge came from practical experience.</note> of small understanding and gifted with little skill, dare to write and teach that which I myself never learned nor was instructed in by anyone else. Nevertheless, because you have repeatedly urged me, and in part persuaded me, to publish my books, I have chosen to risk the danger of **slander** <note>original: "nachred," meaning public gossip or disparaging talk.</note> for your sake.

And although I hope that no one gifted with virtue and understanding will interpret it poorly—that I bring to light, with great diligence, constant effort, and labor, and at no small loss of my own time, something for the common benefit of all artists—I believe most people will praise my goodwill and take it in the best possible way. Since I have no doubt that I will be doing a favor to all lovers of art and those who desire to learn, I must let **envy** (which leaves nothing unpunished) go its usual way. My answer to it is this: it is much easier to criticize a thing than to invent it oneself.

It would certainly be wrong if the books of the ancients <note>Referring to lost classical Greek and Roman texts on art theory, such as those by Apelles.</note> who wrote on the arts of painting were still before our eyes; then, my project might be judged harshly as if I intended to find something better. However, since such books have been entirely lost through the passage of time, I cannot be justly reproached if I publish my opinions and inventions in writing, just as the ancients did. This is so that other intelligent people may be given similar opportunities, and our descendants may have something to increase and improve, so that the art of painting may reach and come to its perfection again in time.

Yet no one is forced to follow this teaching of mine as if it were entirely perfect in every detail. For human nature has not yet declined so far that a painter might not invent something even better. Therefore, anyone may use my instruction as long as it pleases them, or until they find something better. If not, they may well consider that this lesson was written not for them, but for others who desire to take it up. For one must have a very dull wit if one does not trust oneself to discover something further, but instead always stays on the old path, following only others and never attempting to think further for oneself.

Therefore, it is fitting for every intelligent person to follow another in such a way that they do not doubt they could discover something better. If that happens, there is no doubt that this art can, in time, regain its perfection as it had in antiquity. For it is obvious that the German painters are not lacking in skill with their hands and the use of colors, although until now they have lacked the art of **measurement**, <note>original: "meſſung," referring to geometry and the mathematical principles of art.</note> as well as **Perspective** and other such things. Therefore, it is to be hoped that if they acquire these as well, and thus master both practice and theory together, they will in time yield the prize to no other nation. But without correct **proportion**, no figure can ever be perfect, even if it is made with as much diligence as is humanly possible. Although it is not necessary to make all figures, especially very small ones, according to strict measurement—because that would require too much effort—nevertheless, if one understands measurement...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer dedicates his work on geometry and measurement to Willibald Pirckheimer, arguing that while German painters are technically skilled, they need the mathematical foundations used by the ancients to achieve artistic perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Willibald Pirckheimer, geometry, measurement, perspective, German art history, proportion, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>The text is from the preface of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (Vier Bücher von Menschlicher Proportion), published in 1528. It continues his discussion on the necessity of geometric foundations for artists.</meta>

...is correctly taught and brings them into habit, then subsequently every image can be made more easily according to measure. So that this instruction of mine might be better understood, I have previously published a book on measurement—namely concerning lines, planes, solids <term>corpor: three-dimensional geometric bodies</term>, etc.—without which this teaching of mine cannot be fundamentally understood. 

Therefore, it is necessary for anyone wishing to undertake this art to first be well-taught in the art of measurement, and to gain an understanding of how all things should be laid out on the ground and drawn up, just as skillful stonemasons do in daily practice; for without that, he will not be able to fully grasp my instruction. 

No one should be discouraged if they do not understand everything immediately; for what is very easy cannot be very skillful <term>künstlich: possessing high artistic or technical craft</term>. But what is skillful requires diligence, leisure <note>original: "mueß" — the dedicated time and mental space required for study</note>, and labor until it is mastered and learned. It is a wasted effort if much toil and diligence is spent on a false thing; but if it has its correct proportions <term>maß: the system of measurement or scale</term>, it can be criticized by no one, even if it is quite simply made. 

In this instruction, I intend to write only about the external lines of the form and images, and where they should be drawn from point to point, but not at all about internal matters. 

As for how old this art is, who first invented it, in what esteem and dignity it once stood among the Greeks and Romans, and how a good painter or craftsman should be skilled—of these things I have no need to write now. But whoever desires to have knowledge of this, let him read Pliny and Vitruvius <note>Pliny the Elder and Vitruvius were the essential Roman authorities on art and architecture during the Renaissance</note>; thus he will receive sufficient instruction regarding it. 

So that these books of mine might subsequently find a protector, and because I wish to show my inclined will toward your Lordship—on account of the manifold love, friendship, and goodness you have shown me over a long time and in many ways—I have dedicated them to your Excellence, with the request that you might interpret my undertaking in the best light and, as you have always done, remain my **Favorable Lord** and patron. In this I will place my full trust and be diligent to deserve it as best I can.

<meta>This page follows the introduction of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). Dürer is establishing the mathematical foundation for his system, moving from general theory to the practical application of a proportional ruler.</meta>

# Divider of the Crown
### A iij
<meta>Catchword: Soles</meta>

->Beginning of the first book.<-

<image-desc>On the right side of the page, a vertical line is drawn, representing a scale or 'Teiler' (divider). It is marked with ten horizontal lines, numbered 1 through 10 from bottom to top, indicating divisions. The numbers are placed to the right of the vertical line.</image-desc>

Therefore, when I undertake to make a human figure <term>bildnuß</term> <note>Dürer uses 'Bildnuß' to mean a representation, image, or figure of a person.</note>, I employ this method: I take a ruler <term>richtscheyt</term> longer than the figure is. Upon it, I draw a straight line as long as the figure should be, so that one end touches the highest point of the **crown** <term>Scheitel</term> <note>The top of the head.</note> and the other the **soles** <term>soln</term> <note>The bottom of the feet.</note>. And whether a figure be tall or short, I draw for each figure its own separate line and divide it specifically. Therefore, whenever I later name a part by its number <term>zal</term>, I always take it from the length of the whole figure between the crown and the soles. 

I diligently divide this length—which in its total number is now one—into two up to fifty or a hundred parts, as many as I need. I dot them <note>Dürer uses 'punctire' from the Latin 'punctum' (point), meaning to mark points or dots.</note> on the ruler next to the long line, and set them all at the top equal to the height of the crown, and mark them all with their digits <term>cyffern</term>, such as 2, 3, 4, etc. By this method, the smallest numbers result in the longer parts, and the largest numbers result in the shorter parts. <note>In this proportional system, '2' represents 1/2 of the body, '3' represents 1/3, etc. Therefore, a higher number indicates a smaller fraction of the body's total height.</note> Thus, of the whole length, the half part is 2, the third is 3, the quarter is 4, and so forth, as mentioned above. When I need it, I also divide each pre-determined part—whether it be long or short—into as many parts as I wish, even or odd. For he who wishes to measure very precisely must make small parts, as he may not be able to reach everything with one fixed part. 

Afterward, I sometimes combine two or three kinds of numbers together, large or small, even or odd, however I best arrive at the result. Such numbers may also be used and directed according to one's own pleasure, as I have just explained. But for better understanding, I wish to draw the divider now described; this divider must first be made correctly and certainly, otherwise much time and effort would be wasted if I always had to seek out each length of a named part within the length between the crown and the soles.

->How I must [measure] the figure follows hereafter.<-

<image-desc>A diagram consisting of two large, overlapping circles (a vesica piscis). Within the intersection of these two circles, a smaller, more complex intertwined shape is drawn, resembling a knot or a stylized figure-eight.</image-desc>

->A iij Soles<-

<summary>This page introduces Albrecht Dürer's method for creating a proportional scale based on the total height of a human figure, from the crown of the head to the soles of the feet. He explains how to divide this total length into fractions (1/2, 1/3, etc.) to create a ruler that remains accurate regardless of whether the subject is tall or short.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, geometry, human figure, scale, ruler, measurement, anatomy, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page begins the description of a specific body type in Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion." Dürer is moving from idealized figures to more varied, realistic types—in this case, a "stout peasant."</meta>

**K**irstly, I take for myself a stout, peasant-like man <note>German: *Bewrischen man*. Dürer often categorized his proportion studies by social class or physical "stoutness," reflecting a Renaissance interest in human diversity.</note> who shall be seven of his heads long. For this purpose, I draw a straight horizontal line, upon which I place three upright straight lines at the correct distance from one another, each as long as the figure is to be. I use the first for the side view, the second for the frontal view, and the third for the rear view. When these lines stand parallel, I first measure the lengths of the man’s limbs between the crown of the head and the sole of the foot. I mark sixteen <note>The text says *zechzehen* (sixteen), referring to the number of horizontal divisions Dürer uses to track body parts across the three views.</note> lengths on the three upright lines, all with horizontal lines at right angles. These lengths of the limbs, as they are described with their numerical figures, I set outside the image with straight upright lines so that they may be recognized immediately, and through which one may quickly find each length of the limbs.

However, the principal parts of the horizontal lines will be these (though occasionally more or less): The first height is the crown. The next line below it I call the forehead line; the next after that, the eyebrows; then the nose, then the chin, and so on: the heights of the shoulder flesh; the height of the throat pit <note>The small hollow at the base of the neck, known as the suprasternal notch.</note>; the height of the chest; the front of the paps <note>German: *bren*, referring to the pectoral or breast area.</note>; the back of the paps; the nipples; under the breasts; at the waist; at the navel; the hip joint; the end of the hip; the end of the belly; on the genitals; the end of the genitals; the end of the buttocks; the attachment point <note>German: *eynpffung*, literally "grafting" or "insertion," referring here to the fold where the leg meets the torso.</note> in the middle of the upper leg; then the outside above the knee; the middle of the knee; the outside below the knee; the inside below the knee; the end of the outer calf; the end of the inner calf; the height of the instep on the foot; the end of the outer ankle on the shinbone; and finally, at the bottom, the end of the sole.

I will place such words at the ends of the upright lines which indicate the lengths of the limbs, so that all this may be easily recognized. I shall use this same method throughout this entire little book for all the figures.

Now, I first measure the length of the limbs as follows:

From the crown down
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the height of the throat pit: one-10th and one-11th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the height of the shoulder: two-11th parts.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the end of the chin: one-7th part.
The height of the crown on the back of the head is halfway between the crown and the forehead.
From the chin upwards to where the hair begins above the forehead: one-10th part. I divide that part into three equal fields: the top for the forehead, the second for the eyes and nose, and the third for the mouth and chin.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the height of the chest: one-30th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;under the armpits: one-13th part.
From the throat pit downwards
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the nipples: one-10th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;under the breast: one-8th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;at the waist: two-11th parts.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the navel: one-40th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the hip joint: one-30th part.
From the height of the waist downwards
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the end of the hip: one-10th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the genitals: one-8th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the end of the same: one-6th part.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to the end of the buttocks: one-10th and one-11th part.
From the end of the buttocks to the attachment of the leg: one-8th part.
From the sole upwards to the bottom of the ankle on the shinbone: one-28th part.
But from the sole upwards to the highest point of the instep: one-20th part.

Since I have now measured the body of the figure according to its length down to the end of the hip, I will subsequently place the knee joint in its proper location, and—

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating human proportions, focusing on the relationship between the torso, the thigh, and the lower leg. The author uses a geometric "proportional-maker" triangle to determine these measurements.</meta>

[The text begins with a continuation of anatomical observations]

...be longer and stronger than the front parts, as is observed in humans. Here, I take the torso <note>German: *leib*; in this context, the trunk of the body</note> as one long part; although it is assembled from many pieces, it is yet undivided and powerful enough to move the other limbs. These three lengths should maintain a proportion to one another; just as the length of the torso relates to the upper leg <note>the thigh</note>, so should the length of the upper leg relate to the length of the shin. However, I do not use this in all figures. This is how I shall do it:

I draw a triangle a.b.c. Let a.b. be the horizontal side and b.c. be the vertical side, so that b remains a right angle. Then I divide the vertical side b.c. with two points, d and e, into three equal fields, and from angle a, I draw two straight lines to these two points, d and e. From this, I seek the proportion <note>German: *vergleichung*, literally 'comparison' or 'balancing'</note> in this way:

I take a straightedge <note>a ruler without marked increments</note> and mark upon it with two points the length of the torso, between the pit of the throat <note>the hollow at the base of the neck</note> and the end of the hip. Likewise, below that, I mark the end of the outer ankle <note>or bony protrusion</note> and the shin, and I label the top point of the pit of the throat with an **f**, the end of the hip with a **g**, and the end of the shin with an **h**. Then I take this straightedge and place point **f** on line **a.d.** of the aforementioned triangle. Keeping this point in its intended shape, I slide the straightedge back and forth along the line until point **f** touches the top triangle line **a.c.** and the lowest point **h** touches the horizontal line **a.b.**

Once I have done this, the line **a.e.** will intersect the straightedge between **g** and **h**, creating the proportional part alongside the torso’s length; I mark this point with an **i**. Thus it happens that just as length **f.g.** relates to length **g.i.**, so too does length **g.i.** relate to length **i.h.** So, I mark the height of the pit of the throat at point **f**, the end of the hip at point **g**, the middle of the knee at point **i**, and the end of the shin at point **h**.

It should also be noted that when I place my straightedge into the triangle, I tilt it at the top with point **f** toward the vertical side **c.b.** This triangle may well be called a "proportional-maker" <note>German: *dergleicher*</note>. This method can also be used in reverse if one wishes to invert something. Many other useful things are brought into the work through this method by those who can use it correctly. Now I place this height **i** into the figure I will draw later, placing the knee at that same spot; then I measure further.

From the middle of the knee upwards to the top of the knee: a 1/21 part.

<gloss>
a 1/40 part; there I end the knee.
From the middle of the knee downwards:
*   two 1/19 parts; there I end the outer calf.
*   an 1/8 part; there I end the inner calf.
</gloss>

And before I proceed further, I will draw this triangle—the proportional-maker—here opposite.

Next, I determine the length of the arm as follows: from the <term>shoulder joint</term> <note>original: *achßelglid*; the point where the arm meets the torso</note>, which is located at the height of the <term>pit of the neck</term> <note>original: *halßgrübleins*; the suprasternal notch at the base of the throat</note>, to the elbow is two 11th parts <note>The author uses a system where the total body height is divided into equal "parts." Here, the upper arm is 2/11ths of the total height.</note>.

The lengths of the limbs are noted above.

And make the heels at the back a 24th part wide.

Now that I have orderly described and marked all lengths, thicknesses, and widths along the three upright lines, I then draw the figure with lines according to my best judgment. Or, if I am able, I place a well-proportioned man before me and draw the lines according to him; that will always be better than what is made from one’s own imagination <note>original: "eigne sinn", referring to one's internal sense or invention without a model</note>.

It is also especially important to note, when one draws the lines of the man’s figure, how nature has so masterfully made man as if he were made of two pieces: the torso set upon the thighs. On both sides, starting from the hip, a furrow <note>original: "wunnen", referring to an anatomical groove or depression</note> goes down around the belly, and likewise backwards over the buttocks. I will show this in the subsequent sketch <term>auffreissen: a technical term for a construction drawing or architectural plan</term> with a special line, as will be seen hereafter in the side, front, and back views.

I designate this man with an X.

# Study of Human Proportions

-> **Anatomical Diagram of the Male Figure** <-

This page presents a meticulous study of the human body through the lens of geometry and mathematics. The drawing features three human figures overlaid with a dense grid of horizontal and vertical lines. These lines are used to establish a <term>canon of proportions</term> <note>A "canon" is a set of mathematical rules or ratios used by artists to create a "perfect" or "idealized" representation of the human body.</note>.

### Profile Measurements

To the left of the page is a full-body profile of a male figure, facing toward the right. The figure is inscribed with various numerical values and fractions that define the relationship between different body parts.

The measurements recorded here include:
*   4
*   1
*   4
*   15

**Body and Limbs:**
A sequence of measurements continues down the length of the torso and legs, predominantly using the number **8**.

<note>In classical and Renaissance art, the number 8 often refers to the "eight-head" proportion, a system where the total height of the body is exactly eight times the height of the head.</note>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for anatomical drawing, shifting the focus from the proportions of the male figure previously discussed to a specific "sturdy" female type.</meta>

<sig>B</sig>
Following this, I will describe a strong, thick, and sturdy woman <note>original: "hewrisch weyb"</note> whose height is seven heads long, according to the same measure as the man described before. I will use the same order as I did for the man, but instead of the three vertical lines, I will place points in those same locations to mark the length of the limbs. I will no longer draw cross-lines through these points for the thickness or width of the side, front, or back views. Instead, I will write the numerical measurements in figures at the location of the cross-lines by the points, which will indicate how thick or wide the side or front view should be at that spot. I will use this method for all following figures so that the drawing <note>original: "riß"</note> does not become too cluttered.

### Now I first measure the limbs according to their length.
From the crown to the height of the shoulder joints: one 10th part and two 23rd parts.
But from the crown to the pit of the throat: two 11th parts.
From the chin <note>original: "ein"</note> upwards to the end of the chin: one 7th part.
But from the chin upwards to the end of the back of the skull: one 8th part.
But from the chin upwards to the end of the forehead: one 10th part.
This tenth part I divide into three equal parts: in the uppermost I make the forehead; in the second, the nose, eyes, and ears; in the third, the mouth and chin. And under the chin, I make the flesh extend further down.

From the height of the throat-pit to:
The waist <note>original: "weychen"</note>: one 5th part.
Under the breast: one 7th part.
Upon the breasts <note>original: "tütlein"</note>: one 9th part.
Under the front armpits <note>original: "ochsen"</note>: one 1st part <note>Dürer likely means the 1st segment of his scale here</note>.
Upon the height of the breast: one 28th part.

The armpit is always lower in the back than in the front.
To the navel: one 22nd part.
To the end of the hip: one 9th part.

From the waist to:
The end of the belly: one 8th part.
The end of the private parts <note>original: "scham"</note>: two 11th parts.
The end of the buttocks: one 5th part.

From the sole of the foot upwards to the height of the crotch <note>original: "ritz"</note>: one 20th part.
But from the sole to the outer ankle-bone of the shin: one 28th part.
From the end of the ankle-bone upwards to the middle of the knee: two 9th parts.
Or I set the knee using the dividers <note>original: "bergleicher"</note> as I did before; do whichever you prefer. From the middle of the knee upwards: one 30th part—there I end the knee. Likewise, one 30th part below it—there I end the knee at the bottom. From the middle of the knee downwards: one 8th part—there I end the calf. And I make the foot one 6th part long. After that, I make the arm from the shoulder joint to the elbow: two 11th parts. From the elbow to the end of the fingers: one 4th part.

From the end of the fingers backward to the joint of the hand: one 10th part.
Now that I have indicated the length of the limbs, I will next measure the thickness for the side-view figure at all the points of the aforementioned lengths.

### Now always pay attention to the points so
that you do not go astray.
The side-view of the head at the point of the back of the skull I make: 9 parts thick.
But at the point of the forehead: one 14th part and one 15th part.
But the eyebrows also: one 14th part and one 15th part.
But the head and nose: one 8th part.
But the chin and neck also: one 8th part.
Under the neck: one 12th part.
But at the throat-pit: one 10th part.
At the height of the breast: one 7th part.

# Proportions of the Female Figure

<term>A I</term> <note>This designation likely refers to the 'First Category' or 'Type A' of the female form within the author's classification system.</note>

**Anatomical Measurements and Symmetry**

The illustrations on this page demonstrate the mathematical construction of the female body. By using both a profile (side) view and a frontal view, the artist establishes a three-dimensional understanding of human symmetry based on fixed ratios.

**The Proportional Scale**

The figures are annotated with a series of numbers and fractions used to define the "ideal" height and width of various body parts. 

*   **Whole Numbers and Fractions:** The measurements are often written with the word **and** <note>original: "et"</note> to join integers with fractional parts.
*   **The Triangle Symbol:** The symbol **Δ** <note>original: "delta"</note> is utilized as a technical shorthand to denote a specific "part" or subdivision of the total body length.

<term>A I</term>

**Observations on Form and Movement**

Between the two standing figures, there is a specific study of a bent arm. 

<margin>The flexion of the limb</margin> <note>This marginal study shows how the proportions of the arm change when the elbow is bent, providing a guide for artists to maintain accuracy during movement.</note>

The baseline beneath the feet of both figures includes a horizontal measurement, ensuring that the scale remains consistent across different perspectives. Every point on the body—from the crown of the head to the soles of the feet—is meticulously charted to provide a geometric blueprint for the human shape.

Next, I shall describe a woman of balanced proportions to match the man previously described; she shall also be eight heads in height. For this, I will use the established order.

First, I measure the length of the limbs:
From the top of the <term>crown</term> <note>The highest point of the head.</note> to the <term>pit of the throat</term> <note>The small depression at the base of the neck.</note> is a 6th part.
Again, from the top of the crown to the end of the chin is an 8th part.
From the end of the chin upward to the top of the forehead is a 10th part.
This 10th part I also divide into three equal parts—as mentioned before—for the forehead, nose, and chin, to be divided equally.
From the pit of the throat to the <term>waist</term> <note>The soft area of the torso between the ribs and hips.</note> I set a 5th part.
To below the breasts is a 16th part.
To the <term>nipples</term> <note>Archaic: "tutlein."</note> is an 11th part.
To under the breast is a 9th part.
From the waist to the end of the buttocks is an 11th part and a 12th part.
To the end of the genitals is a 13th part to a 14th part.
To the end of the hip is a 10th part.
To the navel is a 40th part.
From the sole of the foot upward to the bottom of the <term>ankle bone</term> <note>Specifically the protrusion of the shinbone.</note> is a 30th part.
To the height of the <term>instep</term> <note>The arched top of the foot.</note> is a 21st part.
To the middle of the knee is an 8th part to a 9th part.
Or I place the knee in its position using the <term>divider</term> <note>A tool like a compass used for measuring proportions.</note>.
From the middle of the knee upward is a 26th part; there I end the knee.
From the middle of the knee downward to the end of the calf, I set an 8th part.

Next, I determine the length of the arm:
From the shoulder joint to the elbow is a 5th part.
From the elbow to the end of the fingers is a 4th part.
From the end of the fingers backward to the wrist joint is a 10th part.

Next, I determine the thickness of the woman from a **side view**:
In the head, across the forehead, is a 9th part.
Across the eyebrows is a 16th part to a 17th part.
Across the nose is two 19th parts.

Across the breast, a 9th part.
Across the nipples, an 8th part.
Under the breasts, a 9th part.

Behind the elbow, a 20th part.
In front of the elbow, a 24th part.

Across the nose, a 12th part.
And the neck under the chin, a 17th part.

In front of the elbow, a 22nd part.

I am altering these two previously made figures as follows, and yet I leave some parts as they were, which I will name in the description as I describe the figure in its entirety. First, the man according to his **length**.

| Under the **chest**: a 9th part. | <margin>Across the chest: an 8th part.</margin> |

Outside above the knee, one 15th part.
Inside above the knee, two 13th parts.
In the middle of the knee, one 18th part.
Outside under the knee, one 19th part.
Inside under the knee, one 19th part.
In the middle of the calf, one 15th part.
At the end of the outer calf, one 16th part.
At the end of the inner calf, one 18th part.
And the shinbone at its thinnest point below,
one 28th part.
At the ankle-slit, one 24th part.
Next, I determine the thickness of the side
arm at the armpit, one 15th part.
Under the armpit, one 20th part.
In the elbow, one 26th part.
At the elbow, one 25th part.
At the joint of the hand, one 48th part.

<meta>This page continues the detailed mathematical proportions for the human figure, likely following a section on the vertical measurements (lengths) of the body. It shifts here to horizontal measurements (widths) for a specific physical type.</meta>

And the hollow of the hand is an 8th part.

Next, I establish the widths of the **front-facing man**:
At the head, across the forehead, an 11th part.
Down at the collarbone <term>collarbone<note>German: haßbein</note></term>, a 10th part.
Across the eyebrows, an 11th part.
Across the ears, an 18th part and a 19th part.
Across the nose, a 12th part.
And the neck under the chin, an 18th part.
At the height of the shoulder muscle <note>referring to the trapezius</note>, a 16th part.
But the pit of the neck <term>pit of the neck<note>German: halßgrüblein; the small depression at the base of the throat.</note></term>, a 6th part.
Below that, the shoulder joints are set apart from one another by two 13th parts.
Across the chest and shoulders, I make the width two 9th parts.
Between the shoulders, a 7th part.
Between the nipples <term>nipples<note>German: tütlein</note></term>, a 9th part.
At the waist <term>waist<note>German: welichen; literally 'the soft parts' or the flanks between the ribs and hips.</note></term>, a 7th part.
Across the navel, a 12th part and two 23rd parts.
At the hip joint, a 12th part and a 13th part.
At the end of the hip, a 10th part and a 12th part.
On this line, the hip joints are set apart from one another by a 15th part and a 16th part.
And I make the leg below the buttocks <term>buttocks<note>German: hindern</note></term> a 12th part wide.
Down at the indentation <note>likely the narrowing of the thigh as it approaches the knee</note>, a 14th part.
On the outside above the knee, an 18th part.
On the inside above the knee, a 19th part.
In the middle of the knee, a 21st part.
On the outside below the knee, also a 21st part.
On the inside below the knee, a 20th part.
In the middle of the calf, a 17th part.
At the end of the outer calf, a 19th part.
At the end of the inner calf, a 21st part.
And the leg at its narrowest point <note>the ankle</note>, a 42nd part.
Through the ankles <term>ankles<note>German: knorren; the bony protrusions of the ankle joint.</note></term>, a 33rd part.
And I make the foot a 19th part wide.

Next, I establish the widths of the **front-facing arm**:
Below the shoulders, a 26th part.
Behind the elbow, a 31st part.
In front of the elbow, a 22nd part.
At the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>, a 38th part.
And the open hand, a 19th part.

Next, I make the man across the back between the shoulders a 7th part wide.
And the heels <term>heels<note>German: farschen</note></term>, a 35th part.
And the buttocks, split from the bottom up, an 11th part.

Once I have marked all these things with points and designated them with numbers, I then draw the figure of the man within them using lines, just as it is sketched hereafter. The designation for this man is a **D**.

If I wish to make the man's head higher than it is there, I take an 8th part of the length between the crown <term>crown<note>German: scheitel</note></term> and the sole <term>sole<note>German: solin</note></term>, and I set this length from that point upwards, and increase the height of the head with it. I leave all other things as they were before, except for the crown at the back of the head, which I place as high as the head was previously. And on the profile head <term>profile head<note>German: nebenfichtigen haubt; literally 'side-viewing head'.</note></term>, I draw the forehead further forward above the eyebrows; in this way, this man will be approximately eight of his heads long, as is sketched out.

*   **The Head:** Labeled with the whole numbers 8, 9, 10, 12, 13, and 17. <note>In Renaissance proportion theory, these often represented "parts" or "minutes" of a larger module, such as the total height of the head.</note>

Following this, I wish to demonstrate a well-proportioned woman according to the measurements described hereafter, also nine heads in length <note>original: *9.lang*; the artist is using the head as a base unit of measurement</note> following the previous order; I will begin first with her height.

From the end of the chin upwards to the end of the forehead: one 10th part.

# Study of the Proportions of the Human Body

->**Profile of the Male Figure**<-

This diagram illustrates the proportional measurements of a standing male figure viewed from the side. The numbers likely refer to a modular system of measurement where the body is divided into fractional parts.

**The Head and Neck**
*   **12** — The crown of the head.
*   **10** — The forehead.
*   **11** — The bridge of the nose.
*   **13** — The base of the chin.
*   **19** — The back of the skull <note>The occipital region</note>.
*   **14** — The width of the neck.

**The Torso and Depth**
*   **10** — The shoulder.
*   **1/10** — Depth of the chest <term>proportional fraction</term> <note>Renaissance artists often used the "head" or "face" as a module; 1/10 often signifies the ratio of a specific part to the total body height.</note>.
*   **1/19** — Depth of the upper back.
*   **10** — Middle of the chest.
*   **1/10** — Depth of the mid-torso.
*   **1/19** — Depth of the lower back.
*   **9** — The curve of the buttock.
*   **7-A** — Width of the hip.

**The Lower Limbs**
*   **10** — Upper thigh.
*   **1/14** — Depth of the upper thigh.
*   **1/16** — Depth of the mid-thigh.
*   **2/21** — The knee.
*   **15** — The calf.
*   **18** — The shin.
*   **19** — The ankle.
*   **16** — The lower leg.
*   **19** — The top of the foot.
*   **28** — The heel.
*   **26** — The ball of the foot.
*   **7** — Total length of the foot base.

### Proportional Measurements for the Rear View

The vertical scale provided on the left of the figure allows the artist to determine the exact placement of muscles and joints from behind. Dürer’s system was revolutionary because it allowed for the construction of a human figure using mathematical ratios rather than just "eye-balling" the anatomy. 

In this specific plate, the focus is on the width of the shoulders and the alignment of the spine and legs. The two smaller heads at the top provide detail on the hair line and the curvature of the skull from the back, ensuring that the head is not merely a flat shape but a three-dimensional volume.

<summary>This page features a woodcut of the human figure from behind, utilizing a vertical scale to define the mathematical proportions of the back, legs, and head.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, back view, proportional scale, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance art, human measurement, woodcut, posterior</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>The transcription is clear. This page continues the systematic breakdown of human proportions, moving from the standard figure to a "long, thin" variation, specifically the ten-head-tall figure. These measurements are likely for a profile or side view, as indicated by the term "seyslichen" (lateral/side).</meta>

Now I wish to create a tall, slender man, and his height shall be ten heads long. I will employ the previously described method, measuring the length and the proportions of the limbs.

From the top of the crown <note>the "scheytel," the highest point of the head</note> to the bottom of the chin, I set one-tenth <note>all fractions refer to portions of the total body height</note>. 
From there upward to the top of the forehead, one-eleventh. 
Within this length, I form the face as previously mentioned. 
But from the top of the crown to the height of the shoulder muscle <note>the trapezius</note>, two-seventeenths. 
To the hollow of the throat <note>the suprasternal notch</note>, one-thirteenth and one-fourteenth. 
To the height of the shoulder joint, one-sixth. 
From the height of the shoulder to the height of the chest, one-twenty-fifth. 
To beneath the armpits <note>original: "ochsen"</note>, one-seventeenth. 
To the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note>, one-thirteenth. 
To beneath the breast, two-twenty-firsts. 
To the waist <note>original: "weychen," the soft part of the flank</note>, two-thirteenths. 
From the waist to the navel, one-thirtieth. 
To the hip joint <note>original: "hüfft art"</note>, one-twenty-first. 
To the end of the hip, one-eighth. 
To the genitals <note>original: "scham"</note>, one-fourteenth and one-thirteenth. 
To the end of the same, two-thirteenths. 
To the end of the buttocks, two-elevenths. 
From the end of the buttocks downward to the narrowing <note>original: "einpeiffung," the indentation of the thigh muscle</note> of the leg, one-eleventh. 
Between the end of the buttocks and the sole of the foot, I place the knee exactly in the middle. 
From the sole upward, one-thirty-fifth; there I end the ankle bone <note>original: "knorn"</note> and the shin bone. 
But from the sole upward, one-twenty-sixth; I set the height of the instep <note>original: "rift"</note> on the foot. 
From the middle of the knee upward, one-thirtieth; there I end the top of the knee. 
But from the middle of the knee downward, one-fortieth; there I end the bottom of the knee. 
Again, from the middle of the knee downward: on the outside, one-tenth, and on the inside, one-ninth. There I end the calves of the leg on both sides. 
And I make the foot one-seventh [of the total height] in length.

Afterward, I make the length of the arm: 
From the shoulder joint to the elbow joint, two-elevenths. 
But regarding the forearm, I will set two options here: first, one-fourth to the tips of the fingers; from there backward to the wrist joint <note>original: "gelenck der hand"</note>, one-eleventh. 
Or [alternatively] from the elbow joint to the end of the hand, three-elevenths; and from the tips of the fingers backward to the wrist joint, two-elevenths.

Afterward, I determine the thickness <note>the depth/width</note> of the man in profile <note>original: "seyslichen," meaning side-view</note> at all the points where the lengths of the limbs are marked. 
In profile, I make the head at the point of the forehead one-thirteenth thick. 
But at the eyebrows, one-eleventh. 
But at the nose and the back of the head, one-eleventh. 
But at the mouth and the neck, one-thirteenth. 
But at [the chin] and the neck, one-fourteenth. 
And the neck under the chin, one-twenty-second. 
At the height of the shoulder muscle, one-twentieth. 
But at the hollow of the throat, one-thirteenth. 
At the shoulder height, one-eleventh. 
At the chest height, two-seventeenths. 
At the armpits, one-eighth. 
But at the nipples also one-eighth. 
Under the breasts, two-seventeenths. 
At the waist, one-eleventh. 
But at the navel also one-eleventh. 
At the hip joint, one-tenth. 
At the end of the hip, one-seventeenth and one-eighteenth. 
At the genitals, one-ninth. 
But the leg at the end of the buttocks, one-eleventh. 
Down at the narrowing of the leg, one-twelfth. 
Above the knee, one-seventeenth. 
In the middle of the knee, one-nineteenth. 
Below the knee, one-twenty-first. 
In the middle of the calf, one-thirty-second and one-thirty-fourth. 
At the end of the outer calf, one-seventeenth. 
At the end of the inner calf, one-seventeenth. 
And I make the leg above the instep one-thirty-second thick. 
Through the instep, one-twenty-ninth.

# Studies in the Proportions of the Human Figure

->[Diagram: The Human Form in Profile and Frontal Views]<-

This page presents a detailed mathematical analysis of the human body, a practice common among Renaissance artists and anatomists who sought to find "ideal" beauty through geometry.

### The Profile View
On the left, a human figure is shown in profile. Along the length of the body, particularly focusing on the **head and neck**, a series of numerical values are recorded: **13, 11**, followed by a repetitive sequence of **1/2**. 

<note>original: "13, 11, 1/2..."</note>
<note>These numbers refer to a system of measurement where the total height of the body is divided into "parts" and "minutes." This allowed an artist to scale a figure to any size while maintaining perfect anatomical harmony.</note>

### The Frontal View and Extremities
The central figure provides a frontal perspective, allowing for the measurement of the width of the shoulders, chest, and hips in relation to the vertical measurements established in the profile view.

To the side, a detached arm is illustrated. 
<note>By isolating the arm, the author can more clearly mark the specific points of articulation—the shoulder, elbow, and wrist—and the proportional length of the hand relative to the forearm.</note>

***

<margin>The measurements recorded here are essential for the student to master before attempting to draw the body in motion.</margin>

### Table of Proportional Divisions
<note>The following represents the calculated ratios implied by the numerical annotations on the figure.</note>

| Body Part | Proportion Value |
| :--- | :--- |
| Total Head Height | 13 units |
| Neck Length | 11 units |
| Sequential Divisions | 1/2 <term>moduli</term> |

<note><term>Moduli</term> are small units of measure based on a single part of the body (such as the length of the nose or the width of an eye) used to calculate the rest of the body's proportions.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates a technical system of human proportions, using numerical fractions to divide the body into measurable segments for artistic or medical study.</summary>

In the following, I will show a woman of equal proportion to the man described previously, and according to the order I have often mentioned, I will first set the points for the length of the body and measure therein the length of the limbs.
