--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing human proportions, moving from general body posture and gender differences to the specific geometric construction of the head.</meta>

**D**Ie <note>Decorated initial 'D'</note> described figures I bend at the joints through the spine <note>original: "ruck grad"</note> and in all other limbs, as any skilled person should know. However, so that an understanding of this may be shown here, I will, for both previously shown figures, first viewed from the side, place the spine in the rear quarter of the body's thickness and mark it with small triangles. Likewise, in the front-facing figures, I will indicate those same joints with small rings. And although I have previously made all the figures the same length here, measured from a divided scale for the sake of less effort, nevertheless, when one wishes to put them into a finished work and arrange the figures together, the thinner figures should always be made longer than the thicker ones.

And when the man and the woman of one sort are arranged together, the line of the woman from which she is measured should be taken a 1/18th part shorter than the man's line is; otherwise, the woman would appear larger than the man, because her naked flesh should be drawn more fleshy and soft <note>original: "molletter," referring to a rounded, supple appearance</note> than the man's, but the man of harder frame and less fleshy.

I have set these wide differences of the previously made proportions of the figures—from the strongest to the weakest—for the reason that anyone may take cause from it, by which means he may change and distinguish his undertaking as he pleases, as I will further show in the following booklets.

As I have previously shown four types of differences in the figures, I will now describe and draw a man's head more clearly from the front; however, I will use the previous order. First, make the head seen from the side within a square <term>vierung<span>a four-sided geometric frame used to plot proportions</span></term>, one-eighth of the man's height in height and also in width.

But the measure for this head I will take in the drawing from a longer line than those in which the aforementioned figures are described, for the sake of greater necessity to show the small parts more clearly.

When I set this square, I mark the front upright side of the plane—where the nose shall protrude—with an **a.**, and the rear upright side with **b.** But the upper cross side with a **c.** and the lower with a **d.** Next, I divide the parts in the face: first according to the length with cross-lines <note>horizontal parallel lines</note> all parallel, and now various letters show the high and low parts of the face.

First, I draw a cross-line above the side **d.** a 1/10th part high, as I mentioned before in the man of eight heads' length. This line divides the hair and the bareness of the forehead at the front; but the rear crown of the head I set a third higher between **c.** and **e.** Next, I divide **e.** and **d.** with two lines **f.** and **g.** into three equal fields. The upper line **f.** shall touch the eyebrows and the top of the ears; but the line **g.** the bottom of the nose at the front, and the head has its indentation at the same place in the back on the neck, as was partly shown before. Next, I divide **e.** and **f.** with two points into three equal fields, and through the second point with a cross-line **h.** This line touches the masculine protrusion of the forehead <note>the brow ridge</note> above the eyebrows; from there the forehead rounds itself upwards until the beginning of the hair. But I divide **f.** and **g.** with two points into three equal fields; the upper third I cut off with a cross-line **J.** <note>the letter J was often used interchangeably with I in this period</note>; thus between **f.** and **J.** the whole eye is contained, and the two corners of the eyes, back and front, in the middle between **f.** and **J.** But I divide **f.** and **g.** with three points into four equal fields; the lowest quarter I cut off with a cross-line **i.** This line touches the top of the nostrils <note>original: "naspellen"</note>; also the lower part and tip is contained between **f.** and **g.** Next, I divide **g.** and **h.** <note>likely a transcription error in the original for g and d</note> with a line **l.** into two equal fields; this line touches the top of the chin, as the **d.** does the bottom. But I divide **g.** and **l.** with a line **m.** in two; this line goes through the middle of the mouth. The upper lip <note>original: "lebſen"</note> I make in the lower third between **g.** and **m.** but the upper...

<summary>Dürer explains the use of geometric markers for joints and the proportional differences between male and female figures before beginning a detailed, step-by-step geometric construction of the human head using a coordinate-like system of lines and letters.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anatomy, geometry, head construction, facial measurements, male vs female anatomy, artistic technique</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues a technical guide on human facial proportions, likely by Albrecht Dürer. The previous page established horizontal coordinates (the x-axis) for facial features; this page concludes the vertical placement of the mouth and introduces vertical parallel lines to measure the "depth" or recession of the face in profile. The catchword "zwen" (two) indicates the next page will likely begin with a measurement involving the number two.</meta>

two-thirds I leave for the groove under the nose <note>The philtrum, the vertical indentation between the nose and the upper lip.</note>. The lower lip I place in the upper half-part between **l** and **m**, but in the lower half-part [I place] the hollow <note>original: "hole keln" — referring here to the mentolabial sulcus, the dip between the bottom lip and the chin.</note> under the lip above the chin. Thus, with these horizontal lines, the most prominent parts of the high and low features of the head are indicated.

Furthermore, I will indicate the depth in the face with upright parallel lines. And by "depths," I mean that which recedes backward from the front, where the nose projects outward. First, I divide the two upright sides **a** and **b** with six upright lines—**n**, **o**, **p**, **q**, **r**, and **s**—into seven equal fields. The first line, **n**, touches the front of the eyeball and the corners of the mouth. And I end the outer corner of the eye in the middle between the upright lines **n** and **o**. For these things should be understood as being applied twofold on both sides [of the face]. After that, I draw a <term>coordinate line</term> <note>original: "ort lini" — a construction line used to determine the specific "place" or "orthographical" point of a feature.</note> from the corner that

<summary>The author explains how to plot the vertical placement of the lower lip and chin indentation using horizontal lines, then introduces a grid of vertical lines to measure how facial features recede from the tip of the nose.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, geometry, drawing techniques, philtrum, anatomy, profile depth, coordinate lines</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geometric head construction, moving from the profile view to the frontal view and explaining how to scale drawings using a triangle.</meta>

First, I draw all the straight, horizontal parallel lines of the first figure <note>original: "zierung"; Dürer uses this to mean a specific view or drawing phase</note> of the profile face, extending them as far as I need in the same order as the letters stand from *c* to *d*. These lines in the frontal face divide all the heights and depths exactly the same as in the profile. Afterward, I draw vertical lines through these horizontal lines; these will indicate all the widths of the parts in the frontal face.

First, I draw two vertical lines *a* and *b* on both sides through the previously drawn horizontal lines, and let them be a 10th part distant from one another. Then, with four vertical parallel lines *c, d, e, f*, I divide the two sides *a* and *b* into five equal fields. In the middle field *d–e*, I place the crown <note>the top of the head</note> in the center between the horizontal lines *c–c*. Then I place the nose in the middle, so that its nostrils *d–e* touch between the horizontal lines *e–g*. I also let the corners of the mouth touch the vertical lines *d–e* on the horizontal line *n*, and the chin <note>original: "Eyn"; refers to the point of the chin</note> remains at the bottom, also between the vertical lines *d–e*. Next, I set the two eyes so that their four corners touch the four vertical lines *c, d, e, f* between the horizontal lines *f–j*. Then I divide the vertical spaces *a–c* and *f–b* with two vertical lines *g* and *h* in the middle. These two lines *g* and *h* touch the height of the cheeks on each side at the horizontal line *f*, and the eyebrows end on both sides in the middle between the vertical lines *a–c* and *f–h* below the horizontal line *f*. Furthermore, I divide *a–g* and *h–b* in the middle with two vertical lines *i* and *k*. These two vertical lines separate the bareness of the forehead from the hair on both sides between the horizontal lines *e–f*, but the head exceeds these two lines on both sides so that half of the ears are covered between the horizontal lines *f–g*. I also draw the two ears on both sides between *a–i* and *k–b* between the horizontal lines *f–g*. But above, at the horizontal line *f*, they exceed the two vertical sides *a–b*, and toward the inside, they taper at the bottom with their lobes <note>original: "zipffellen"</note> toward the two vertical lines *i–k*.

Once the vertical lines have been drawn through the horizontal lines—which indicate all widths, heights, and depths in the frontal face—I then draw the contour lines of the face into this division. First, the head from the top, where the horizontal line *e–c* touches the middle between the two vertical lines *d–e*; from there, it runs down on both sides to the vertical lines *a* and *b* at the horizontal line *h*, then down to the ears at the horizontal line *f*, centered between *a–i* and *k–b*. Then I draw the bareness of the forehead and the cheeks <note>original: "Einpacken"</note> through the two vertical lines *g* and *h* on the horizontal line *m* all the way down to the chin. And then, from the previously mentioned ends, I draw the neck down from both sides onto the horizontal line *d*. There I make it a 15th part wide, or a little wider. Afterward, I draw in the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin along with the most important features that the face requires.

When that is finished, I make the third figure <note>view</note> in there: the back of the head, following the outer lines of the frontal view, and I place the back of the crown in its position and draw the rounding of the head onto the neck.

If I wish to lay out this above-described head on a ground plan, I do so by means of a triangle <note>original: "triangl"</note> which I will demonstrate hereafter, so that from one given scale I may transfer the same into another changed scale, yet keeping each one uniform. I mark this triangle with 1, 2, 3; it should always have a right angle so that it may be used on all sides as one requires, whether it has equal or unequal sides, long or short. Within it, I take whichever side I wish for myself and mark it with points, such as 4, 5, 6, etc. From these, I draw parallel lines against one side of the triangle toward the third side; thus they divide that side in the same way the first was divided. If I then proceed again from the newly created points with parallel lines toward the undivided side, it will also be divided uniformly like the first and the second.

<summary>This page provides a highly technical, step-by-step geometric guide for constructing a human face in a frontal view using a grid of vertical and horizontal lines, concluding with a method for scaling proportions using a right-angled triangle.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, facial proportions, Albrecht Dürer, construction lines, frontal view, scaling, anatomy in art, grid system</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's geometric investigations into the human form, specifically focusing on the mathematical projection of the head from three-dimensional space onto a two-dimensional ground plan.</meta>

### E ij

From this figure, many different things can be made, and for my purpose here, it may well be called a **transfer** <note>original: *vbertrag*; here referring to a geometric projection or a method of transferring dimensions from one view to another</note>, for by this way one may carry and draw any one thing into another. But before I press the head down into the **ground-plan** <note>original: *nider inn grund drucke*; this refers to creating a top-down or orthographic projection, essentially "flattening" the three-dimensional object onto a horizontal plane</note>, I wish to draw this transfer here below.

---

-> <note>A geometric diagram consisting of a right-angled triangle with a grid of horizontal and vertical lines, and several diagonal lines originating from the hypotenuse toward the right angle. It is labeled with numbers 1, 4, 5, 6, 2 along the top and 3 at the bottom vertex. The words "The transfer" are printed inside the triangle.</note> <-

---

And so I use the transfer for this purpose. I place the **side-view** <note>original: *nebenſichtig*</note> and **frontal** <note>original: *fürwertig*</note> head before me, as described in their **frames** <note>original: *vierungen*; literally "squarings," referring to the grid-boxes used to measure proportions</note>, and first draw all vertical lines—a. n. o. p. q. r. s.—straight downwards from the square of the side-view head as far as I need them. After that, I also draw all vertical parallel lines—a. i. g. c. d. e. f. h. k. b.—straight downwards from the other square of the front-facing countenance, as mentioned before.

Then I draw a straight horizontal parallel line below the two squares of the head, through all the vertical lines that were drawn down from above. Where this **horizontal line** <note>original: *zwerchlini*</note> intersects the two vertical sides (a. b.) of the front-facing countenance, I place the number **1** at line a. and **2** at line b. Afterward, I measure from the number 1 on line a. downwards, as wide as the square of the front-facing countenance is; there I place **3** and connect 2 and 3 together with a **diagonal line** <note>original: *ortlini*; a line used to determine perspective or projection points</note>. Thus, this triangle for the flattened ground-plan is finished, which I call the transfer.

Next, where this diagonal line 2–3 intersects the vertical lines drawn down from the frontal head, I move straight across from those same points with horizontal parallel lines through all the vertical lines drawn down from the side-view head. Where the horizontal line 2–1 and the horizontal line 3 intersect the two vertical sides (a. b.) of the side-view countenance, I place **4** and **5** at the front of these corners of the square, and **6** and **7** at the back. This creates an **oblong square** <note>a rectangle</note> in which the head is contained within the flattened ground-plan.

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric construction of a 'transfer' triangle used to project the dimensions of a human head from frontal and profile views into a flattened ground-plan (orthographic projection).</summary>
<keywords>geometry, perspective, human proportion, projection, ground-plan, Albrecht Dürer, technical drawing, transfer triangle</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical explanation of how to map the human head onto a geometric grid, moving from basic proportions to complex spatial projections.</meta>

Now, once the <term>squares</term> <note>original: "vierung"; referring to the units of the measurement grid</note> 4, 5, 6, and 7 have been drawn through the vertical lines with the help of the <term>transfer</term> <note>original: "übertrag"; the process of projecting points from one view to another</note> for both the profile and frontal views, these small squares will indicate where every section of the face and its defining lines should be placed. Each square contains the exact location of features such as the eyes, nose, mouth, chin, ears, neck, and all the lines belonging to the form, just as I have subsequently drawn them with lines. I have drafted <note>original: "aufgerissen"</note> everything precisely: first the head in <term>profile</term> <note>original: "nebensichtig"</note>, then <term>frontally</term> <note>original: "fürwertig"</note>, then from the <term>back</term> <note>original: "hinderwertig"</note>, and finally in a <term>plan view</term> <note>original: "nidergedruckten grund"; literally a "lowered ground," essentially a top-down cross-section</note>. 

It is useful to know that if this head were made of wax and cut along any <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerchlini"</note>, one could know beforehand exactly what shape that specific <term>plane</term> <note>original: "planus"</note> would have; this is discovered through this method. 

If I wish, however, to make the head one-seventh of the total body height <note>Dürer is discussing a "shorter" canon of proportion</note>, whereas in the previous figures it was one-eighth high, I still leave all the other parts of the face as they were before. I only heighten and thicken the head on the sides, from the forehead upwards and then backwards down to the neck, between the horizontal lines **g** and **m**; however, I make the neck two 27th-parts thick. 

Afterward, I broaden the frontal view of the head in order so that it becomes approximately two 17th-parts wider across the forehead, and I draw it back in on both sides above the ears to the two vertical sides **a** and **b**. I also heighten and widen the bare parts of the forehead and pull the tops of the ears further away from the head, as will be shown in the following <term>diagram</term> <note>original: "aufreissen"</note>. It should be noted that if a figure is correctly divided into seven of its own head-lengths, it is a proportion not to be disregarded.

In the same way that I have placed the head into a <term>plan view</term>, one may also place an entire figure—whether it stands straight or contorted—into a plan view.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting human proportions, focusing specifically on the geometry of the hand. It follows previous sections likely detailing the arm or the body as a whole, maintaining the 1/10th ratio of the hand to the total body height.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>
<sig>E iij</sig>

Further, I wish to describe more fully the outstretched hand of a strong man, as it was indicated previously. And just as I previously made it a 10th part <note>A standard Renaissance proportion where the hand length equals 1/10th of the total body height.</note> in length, I shall let it remain so here. For the length of this 10th part, I set a vertical line before me, marking it at the top with an **a.** and at the bottom with **b.**, and I enclose it between two parallel <term>horizontal lines (zwerch linien)</term> **c.** and **d.** After that, I divide **a. b.** with 18 points into 19 equal fields. Then I draw another vertical line between the horizontal lines **c. d.**, parallel to the vertical line **a. b.**, far enough away so that the hand I intend to make remains unobstructed. Let this line be **e.** at the top and **f.** at the bottom; I place this in the middle of the width of the outstretched hand, so that it passes through the long finger <note>The middle finger.</note>. For I draw the hand vertically here, so that at the top, the longest finger touches the horizontal line **c.**, but at the bottom, I let the line **d.** pass through the middle of the wrist joint. Then, from point **1.**, I draw a horizontal line **g.**, which touches the end of the <note>original: "golt fingers"</note> ring finger <note>Literally "gold finger," the traditional name for the finger on which a wedding ring is worn.</note> at the top. After that, I place a point on the line **a. b.** midway between **1.** and **2.**; from there I draw a line **h.**, which touches the end of the index finger at the top. Then I draw from point **4.** a horizontal line **j.**, which touches the top of the last part of the little finger. Furthermore, I draw from point **7.** a horizontal line **k.**, which touches the end of the thumb.

Once I have indicated the ends of the fingers, I mark their hindmost <term>joints (glyd)</term> where they meet the hand. I draw from point **11.** a horizontal line **l.**; this passes through the two hindmost joints of the index and long fingers, for they stand at the same height next to each other at the front of the hand. Thus, the long finger becomes longer than the entire other part of the hand. And where the line **l.** intersects the vertical line **e. f.**, I place an **m.** Thereafter, I set a compass with one foot on point **f.** and the other on point **m.**; from there, I strike a curve backward out of the hand as far as I need. In this curved line, I place the two hindmost joints of the ring finger and the little finger, for they sit deeper in the hand than the front two. Further, I draw from point **14.** a horizontal line **n.**; this goes through the middle of the hindmost joint of the thumb. Now that the length of the hand and all the fingers are indicated, I will further show the finger joints. I draw from point **10.** a horizontal line **o.**, which goes through the middle of the second joint of the thumb. After that, I divide the index finger lengthwise between **h.** and **l.** with a horizontal line in the middle; this goes through the middle of its second joint. Then I divide the front half lengthwise with 6 points into 7 equal fields and draw a horizontal line from point **3.**; this goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the <insert>aforementioned</insert> finger. Then I draw from point **6.** a horizontal line **p.**, which goes through the second joint of the long finger. Then I divide between the horizontal lines **c.** and **p.** with 6 points into 7 equal fields and draw from that same point **3.** a horizontal line; this goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the long finger. The horizontal line **k.** goes through the middle of the second joint of the ring finger. Then I divide the forward length with 8 points into nine equal fields and draw from that same point **4.** a horizontal line; this goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the aforementioned finger.

I make the little finger as long as the two rear parts of the long finger are, or I let it remain as it turned out previously. This length I divide with 10 points into 11 equal fields, and I draw from point **6.** a horizontal line; this goes through the middle of the second joint of the little finger. After that, I divide the 6 upper parts anew with ten points into eleven equal fields and draw from the newly created point **5.** another horizontal line, which goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the aforementioned finger. Let everyone take careful note of the numbers taken between **a.** and **b.** or from the other smaller parts.

I make the nails on the fingers each half as long as the part of the joint on which they sit. Beside the longest finger on both sides, the split between the fingers goes to the half-point of the hand's length, but between the ring and little finger, the split goes deeper.

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the hand's proportions, following the measurements of length established on the previous page. The text moves from lengths to widths and thicknesses, utilizing a coordinate system (a, b, d, n, l) visible in the accompanying diagram.</meta>

...into the hand; but the cleft between the thumb goes even deeper into the hand.

Since I have now described the length of the parts in the hand, I will then indicate the thickness and width of those same parts. And although I have already described their thickness from the side earlier with the figures, it would not fit well here with the hand; therefore, I will now describe the outstretched hand according to its width, and only then its thickness from the side.

I make the flat hand at the joint on the <term>transverse line</term> <note>original: "zwerchlini"; a horizontal reference line used in Dürer's coordinate system</note> **d.** seven 19th parts wide of the length **a.b.** But on the transverse line **n.** eleven 19th parts wide, because the hand is widest at the <term>knuckle</term> <note>original: "knorren"; here referring to the protuberance or ball of the thumb</note> of the thumb. And the other width from the outside by the index finger on the transverse line **l.**—passing through the hindmost joints of the fingers to the end of the circular space, outside at the end of the smallest finger—I make the hand as wide as the index finger is long.

I make the longest finger one-fifth part of its own length wide at the back, and at the front, one-fourth of its width narrower than at the back. I also make the index finger just as wide at the back as the long finger, but at the front I make it a little narrower than the long finger. I make the <term>gold finger</term> <note>original: "gold finger"; the traditional name for the ring finger</note> and the smallest finger each one-fifth wide of its own length, and make each one a fourth narrower at the front than it is at the back. I make the thumb at its middle joint one-third wide of its own length.

The fingers stand at the back of the hand, each in the center of its width; there, I will draw small circles in the sketch.

It should be noted that in the hand, no finger has the same form as another. Let anyone examine this in people, and he will find it to be so. I also observe that when the hand is outstretched, it does not stand quite straight with itself, but shifts outward at the small finger. Next, I make the thickness of the hand from the side: at the joint of the arm, five 19th parts of the vertical length **a.b.** But through the knuckle of the thumb, on the transverse line **n.**, it is half as thick as the flat hand is wide; likewise further down in the palm it is just as thick. However, the hand forms a hollow, because the two sides of the thumb and small finger stand inward; therefore, the hand is not as thick as it appears or as the outer lines indicate.

The thumb in its foremost joint is just as thick as the foremost part of the smallest finger is long; but further toward the front, it is one-fourth thinner, though at the back toward the knuckle, it is quite thick. The fingers are approximately as thick as they are wide according to their measurements, except in the joints where it varies a little.

Now that I have recorded all the lengths, widths, and thicknesses of the hand, I then draw the shape of the hand with lines into it, as I have sketched hereafter. Whoever copies this, let him take care that he misleads nothing, for these lines are difficult to draw correctly.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer details the proportional widths and thicknesses of the human hand, using a 19-part scale relative to the hand's length and emphasizing the hand's natural hollow and asymmetrical alignment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing the proportions of the human body, specifically focusing on the anatomical measurements of a "strong man" (the robust or "herculean" type) as established in the previous sections of Dürer's treatise.</meta>

<sig>E iiij</sig>

Furthermore, I wish to describe the feet of a strong man more clearly than before, as I previously mentioned regarding the man who is made eight head-lengths tall, whose foot I described as one-sixth long and one-seventeenth wide. <note>Dürer refers here to his "Type B" or robust male figure, using the "head" as the basic unit of measurement.</note>

From this length and width, I draw an **oblong rectangle**, placing it horizontally on a level surface before me. Within this, I draw the foot so that it touches both sides at the back and the front, as follows. I draw the outline of the foot and mark the farther long side with an **a** and the side nearer to me with a **b**. The two short sides I mark as follows: the front, where I intend the toes to extend, with a **c**, and the back with a **d**. In this oblong rectangle, I place the foot so that it lies entirely flat in a low-profile ground plan.

Then, I first divide this rectangle according to its length between **c** and **d** with two lines, **e** and **f**, into three equal fields, so that **e** stands at the front. <note>To avoid confusion, Dürer specifies that the letters mentioned first are placed at the forward positions.</note> Note that although the letters are placed throughout the diagram, the ones mentioned first should always be set forward at that time, as stated above. In the front field between **c** and **e**, I shall place the toes; in the middle field between **e** and **f**, I shall make the <term>instep</term><note>The "bare foot" or bridge between the toes and ankle.</note>; and in the hinder third field, I shall place the heel and the leg above the foot.

Next, I divide line **d** with six points—**g, h, i, k, l, m**—into seven equal fields. From point **g**, I draw a parallel line to the foremost line **c**. Where it touches, I place an **n**; where it intersects the upright line **f**, I place an **o**. Then I divide **l** and **m** with a point **p** in the middle. From this point **p**, I draw a parallel horizontal line to line **f**; in that same point, I place a **q**. Then I draw a <term>slanted line</term><note>original: "schleme lini," meaning an oblique or diagonal line.</note> **k q**. 

Afterward, I place a point **r** on line **c** midway between **a** and **b**. Then I divide **r b** with two points, **S** and **T**, into three equal fields. I also divide **b e** with two points, **v** and **x**, into three equal fields. Then I again divide **e f** with two lines, **y** and **z**, into three equal fields, and divide **d** with two points, **st** and **ß**, into three equal fields. Where line **g** intersects line **e**, I place a **tz**. Then from point **h**, I draw a horizontal line to line **f**; where it touches, I place an **a. i.** Then I divide **r S** with two points, **1** and **2**, into three equal fields. I also divide **r c** with two points, **1** and **2**, into three equal fields. Then I divide **ß d** with four points—**1, 2, 3, 4**—into five equal fields. From point **3**, I draw a line to the horizontal **h a. i.**; there I place **b. i.** It should also be noted that I have placed all the letters of the short lines upon the horizontal **a**. From these, I draw several slanted lines: one **e n**, the other **r tz**. Another from point **2** (between **r S**) to point **y**. Then one **t l**, then **b o**, and then **b a. i.**

Next, I draw a straight line from point **st** to the slanted line **k q**; there I place **e. i.** Then I set a <term>compass</term><note>original: "circkel," the standard tool for geometric measurement and drafting in the Renaissance.</note> with one foot on point **tz** and draw with the other foot from point **n** out of the foot to the slanted line **r tz**. Then I leave the one foot of the compass standing, and without moving it, I draw a curved line between the slanted lines **tz** and **b o**. Then, leaving the compass foot still at point **tz**, I close it tighter and set the other foot at point **2** on line **b** between **x** and **e**, and from there draw forward to the slanted line **b o**. Then I leave the compass foot at point **tz** and set the other foot at point **r**, drawing from there to the slanted line **t z**. 

Now that I have indicated with slanted and curved lines where I will place the toes and where they are cut off at the front, I shall furthermore place their hindmost joints. I place a point on line **e** midway between the slanted lines **b o** and **b a. i.** In this point, I will place the hindmost joint of one of the smallest toes. I set a compass with one foot in point **3** and the other in the point just made; from there, I draw a curve to the horizontal line **g n**, where...

Here I place the back-most joint of the big toe; afterward, on this circular arc, I place two points midway between the <term>slanted lines</term><note>Dürer uses the term "schlemen" to refer to the diagonal or oblique construction lines in his geometric grid.</note> **s, n, r, z,** and **b, o.** There I position the two back-most joints of the middle toes; I will sketch them according to their sizes, marking them with elongated rings. The back-most joint of the little toe I place with a point on line **e,** midway between the <term>cross-line</term><note>original: "zwercher"</note> **b** and the slanted lines **b** and **a, i.** The big toe has two joints, and I make the rear part a little longer than the front.

The three middle toes I each divide into three joints, so that the back-most part is as long as the front two parts combined. However, each front-most part of these three middle toes is one-quarter longer than the second part behind it—not because of the inner bone, but because of the flesh they have in front of them. For the smallest toe, I create a strong "rear ball" at its back-most joint and let it end in front at line **r.** I make its back-most part longer than the front ones. I make every nail on all the toes half as long as the segment on which it sits. 

Next, I place one foot of the compass on point **n** and the other foot a little above the point of the back-most joint of the little toe on line **e.** From there, I draw a line through the <term>shin-lines</term>, and where the curve intersects the slanted lines, I draw short lines from those points as markers. This is how far I split the toes into the flesh, although on the foot itself, I draw the <term>furrows</term><note>original: "arten" (referring here to the grooves or veins)</note> and hollows much further back. Line **f** touches the front of the <term>instep</term><note>original: "rist"</note>, from where the bone pulls upward; but beneath the step, the cut extends two-thirds past line **f** toward **z.**

Furthermore, I divide **b, d** with four points into three equal fields, and from point **z,** I draw a straight line that touches the <term>tendons</term><note>original: "spann'adern"</note> at the back, above the heel.

Now that I have drawn all the straight, slanted, and curved lines through which the foot is entirely <term>modeled</term><note>original: "gepossiert," meaning to form or shape in a three-dimensional sense</note>, I then draw the contour line showing how it should lie on the flattened ground, beginning at the front.

First, I draw the big toe with its ball coming forward from point **e,** outside the slanted line **e, n,** until nearly the middle of the back part of the toe; then I move in a curve under the slanted line **e, n** and pull the contour line back to the slanted line **e, n** before the front-most joint. From there, I bring the toe fully forward to the circular arc midway between **n** and **r.** From there, I draw it backward as far as it is split, up to line **r, t, s.** 

Afterward, I draw the three middle toes between their slanted lines so that they touch the circular arcs at the front. Regarding this, it should be noted that the big toe should not stand out completely straight, as is found among many coarse people; for this reason, I have drawn the slanted lines so that the toes are positioned more outward toward the little toe, for the sake of strength and beauty. Therefore, I leave a space between the largest and the next toe, as the middle three toes should stand more outward than the largest. However, I draw the smallest toe inward toward the other toes of the slanted line **b, a, i.** I end it on the outside at line **e,** two-thirds from its back joint between line **b.** I also draw the front-most toe the largest, but the following ones each smaller than the last, showing how the <term>knuckles</term><note>original: "knözlein"</note> and other parts should have their shape, each one different from the other. Then I draw the outside of the foot from the ball of the little toe into the angle **b, y.**

Next, I draw the inner line of the foot from point **e,** specifically curving it toward line **y,** midway between the long side **a** and the line **n, g.** I continue drawing it backward, curving inward until line **f,** midway between **f** and **o.** From there, I go all the way back and round the heel to line **d** at point **h.** From there, I draw the heel...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer provides meticulous geometric instructions for drawing the human foot, focusing on the proportional segments of the toes and the placement of joints. He emphasizes that the toes should slant slightly outward for aesthetic "beauty" and structural strength, rather than pointing straight forward.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, geometry, foot construction, toe joints, artistic theory, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

<meta>image quality: excellent, catchword: schin- (referring to the continuation of the word "Schinbein" or shinbone). This page continues the technical instructions for drawing the human foot using a geometric grid system, a method perfected by the Renaissance artist Albrecht Dürer.</meta>

...how it has an unusual curved line as far as the slanting lines k. q. However, by the line s. t., I draw a curve into the foot, but from point q., I draw fully to the ball of the foot behind the small toe at points y. b. I mark these soles on the inside with special small dots on the line s. t. Through the cut in the <term>instep</term><note>original: "rist," the top part of the foot between the toes and the ankle</note> above both <term>ankle bones</term><note>original: "knorren," the medial and lateral malleoli</note> of the **shinbone**, I make the width a 26th part; but under the ankle bones, I make it a 31st part. I then draw the section forward to line f., two-thirds of the way toward line z. I also draw the inner ankle bone flatter and the outer one more pointed; likewise, the inner one further forward and the outer one further back. In this way, the hollow next to the <term>tendon</term><note>original: "spanaderen," likely referring to the Achilles tendon or the tendons of the long peroneal muscle</note> behind the inner ankle bone becomes longer and flatter than the outer one. After that, I draw a specific ball on the outer side closest to the bone, from line f. to line z.; this gives the foot a good appearance. Then I draw the ball behind the little toe through line y. up to the halfway point toward line z. In such drawing of the contour lines, one must take great care to maintain the proper shape so that nothing is spoiled. After that, I draw the two cross-sections through the feet e. f. separately, showing what form they have on a flat plane so that all of this may be perceived more clearly; for just as I demonstrate these two sections, one may seek out the sections in all bodies, whether they be straight or slanting.

Next, I will elevate the foot from the <term>ground plan</term><note>original: "grund," the bird's-eye view or footprint</note> to a height of one 24th part above the flattened view. By transferring from the flattened ground plan, I draw the lines c. e. y. z. f. d. as they stood before, and whatever else is necessary, all in an upward direction. Through these elevated lines, I draw two <term>transverse lines</term><note>original: "zwerch linien," horizontal grid lines</note> at a height of one 24th part; this creates an elongated quadrangle in which I place the elevated foot. Then I divide the transverse side a. b. with two other horizontal lines e. f. into three equal fields. Thus, the first elevated lines indicate where all parts of the foot belong according to their length, such as the foremost sections of the toes. Next come their joints and the gaps between them, and also where the nails should stand, where the foot is placed with all its features and balls, and likewise how the bone above the heel is elevated in its section. But through the transverse lines, I indicate the heights of the foot and its parts, placing the soles flat upon line b. 

Furthermore, I divide the vertical line c. between the transverse lines f. and b. with two points, 1. and 2., into three equal fields; but I divide the vertical line e. between the transverse lines e. and f. with three points, 1. 2. 3., into four equal fields. Then draw a transverse line midway between the horizontals a. and e., between the vertical lines z. and d. Then I draw the contour lines of the foot into these markings; I begin at the corner a. f. and draw the instep down to the vertical line e. at point 3. From there, I draw the ball behind the big toe down to the transverse line f. Then I continue forward beneath it to point 1., where the big toe ends at the front and top; but the smallest toe touches the fourth part between f. and b. at the top with its hindmost joint and ball. Then I draw the three middle toes in order, making the longest one the highest, and each one further forward lower than the last, making the smallest toe quite low at the front. After that, I draw its ball behind the vertical line y. But I draw the side-ball of the foot so that it touches the transverse line e. at the top and ends at the bottom in the upper third between the transverse lines f. and b., and touches the corner of the horizontal f. and vertical z. at the front, ending behind the vertical s. t. between the horizontals e. and f. After that, I draw the heel so that it touches the vertical d. on the horizontal f., and draw it at the top into the corner enclosed by the horizontal e. and the vertical z., which I have drawn from its point on the horizontal a. between z. and d. From there, I draw the bone upward at the back. Then I draw the outer ankle bone of the **shinbone** from point 3. backward and down to the middle horizontal line between a. and e. And above the instep, over the ankle bones of the **shinbone**, I make the bone a 24th part thick on the side, and draw the shin-

<summary>This page provides meticulous geometric instructions for drawing the human foot in both plan and elevation views, utilizing a 24th-part proportional system. Dürer details the specific placement of the ankles, toes, and heel within a grid to ensure anatomical accuracy and "wolstand" (aesthetic beauty).</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, foot construction, geometry, Renaissance art theory, shinbone, ankle, instep</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting the lower extremities, specifically focusing on the anatomical proportions of the foot and ankle, following the lateral views discussed previously.</meta>

...the leg from the points of the vertical line **f** upwards and backwards, and the curve of the heel I draw down from the horizontal line **e** above and forward.

Following this, I also want to show the foot from a rear view a little bit; for that, I create a square as high and wide as the foot is, and from the transfer <note>This refers to projecting measurements from one drawing—like a side view—onto another.</note> I draw the points and lines into it. These divide and show all parts of the foot, such as how the inner <term>ankle bone</term> <note>original: "knozr", referring to the medial malleolus</note> should stand higher and the outer one lower. And the widths in the <term>instep</term> <note>original: "rist", the arched top part of the foot</note> through the ankle bones I make one 26th part <note>Dürer measures the body in fractions of the total height; here, the ankle width is 1/26th of the person's full height.</note>; and on the midline between the horizontal lines **a** and **e**, one 31st part. But the leg above both ankle bones I make one 34th part wide.

Also, the foot is to be made from the front using the same method as the rear view. I have <term>drafted</term> <note>original: "auffgerissen", a term for technical or architectural drawing</note> this intention of mine very specifically hereafter, and showed it more in the <term>drawing</term> <note>original: "reyssen"</note> than in writing, from which it will be very easily understood how this third base plan should be made from the first two base plans already created.

<summary>This page explains how to geometrically construct rear and front views of the foot using a proportional grid. Dürer provides specific fractional measurements for the ankle and instep, noting that the inner ankle must be drawn higher than the outer one.</summary>
<keywords>foot anatomy, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometric drafting, ankle height, Renaissance art theory, measurement systems</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric exploration of human anatomy, moving from the proportions of the feet to the construction of the female head. It maintains the same mathematical rigor established in the previous sections regarding the male figure.</meta>

**SINCE** the foot has been changed <note>original: "geendert"; Dürer is transitioning from his previous chapter on the proportions of the feet</note>, I will henceforth describe a woman's head more precisely. I shall use the same method there as was used for the previous description of the male head. Just as I previously took an eighth part of the figure's total length for the height of the female head in the other types of figures, I will also adopt a height of one-eighth here, making it the same size in measure as the man’s head.

First, I place three quadrangles <term>Vierung: literally 'foursquare,' referring here to the rectangular bounding boxes used for geometric drafting</term> next to each other. By transferring the measurements, I make each one an eighth of the body's length in height. The first is one-eighth wide, and in it, I draw the head from the side <note>a profile view</note>. The other two quadrangles—one for the front-facing head and the other for the head seen from behind—I make each a tenth of the body's length in width. At the beginning, I mark the two upright sides of the quadrangle in which I draw the profile head: the front side, where I will place the nose, with an **a**, and the back side with **b**. Then, with other vertical lines between **a** and **b**, I divide all the depths found in the face, marking each with a specific letter. The numbers I use to describe them are taken from the total length of the figure. 

Now, I set the first line **c** an 80th part behind **a**. This line **c** touches the crown of the head above the forehead where the hair begins, and the eyebrows end at the front of it (for they should not touch each other above the nose). It also touches the upper lip at the front. Next, I set another line **d** a 64th part backward—understand this is always measured from the front line **a**. This line touches the front of the upper eyelids, the lower lips of the mouth, and the front of the chin. Then I draw a line **e** a 56th part backward; this touches the hollows above the eyelids, the front of the eyeball, the back of the nostril <term>naspellein: literally 'nasal skin,' referring to the wing or flare of the nostril</term>, the corner of the mouth, and the groove <note>the philtrum or the indentation under the lip</note> under the lower lip. Next, I set a line **f** backward a 40th part; it touches the back corner of the eyes. Furthermore, I set a line **g** backward a 32nd part; it touches the back ends of the eyebrows and, at the bottom, the hollow under and behind the chin. After that, I set a line **h** backward a 22nd part; it touches the front of the neck. Further, I set a line **i** a 14th part backward; it touches the inner ear. Then I draw a line **k** backward an 11th part; the back of the ear ends at this line, though the lower earlobe remains halfway toward line **i**. Then I set a line **l** a 9th part backward; it touches the back crown of the hair on the head and the nape at the very bottom of the head; from there, I draw the neck sloping downward and backward. Finally, I draw the last upright parallel line **m** two-17th parts backward; it touches the middle of the back of the neck.

Once I have drawn all the vertical lines and indicated what they should touch, I then divide the heights and depths of the features in the face with parallel horizontal lines <term>zwerch linien: cross-lines or horizontal coordinates</term>. 

First, let the uppermost horizontal line of the aforementioned quadrangle be an **n**; it touches the highest part of the head, and the lowest line shall be **x. y.** Then I draw a horizontal line **o** an 80th part below line **n**; it touches the back crown of the head. Then I draw a line **p** down from **n** a 30th part; it touches the front hairline <term>harwachs: where the hair grows out from the forehead</term> above the forehead. Then I draw a line **q** a 16th part below **n**; it touches the top of the eyelid, the top of the ears, and the eyebrows end upon it at the back and front—though in the middle, I draw them slightly above it. Then I draw a line **t** midway between **q** and **y**; it touches the bottom of the nose, and there the nape of the neck meets the head. 

Next, I draw a line **r** and cut away the uppermost third between **q** and **t**; it touches the bottom of the eyelid. The two corners of the eyes I place in the middle, as well as the eyeball between **q** and **r**. After that, I cut away the lowest fourth between **q** and **t** with a line **s**; it touches the top of the back of the nostril and the bottom of the ears. Then I divide **t** and **y** with a line **f** in the middle; it touches the top of the chin. Then I divide **t** and **r** with a line **b** in the middle; it goes through the middle of the mouth. Then I divide **t** and **b** into three equal fields; in the bottom one, I make the upper lip, and in the upper two, I make the hollows <note>the philtrum</note> in front under the nose above the mouth. Between **b** and **f**, I make two parts; in the upper one, I make the lower lip, and in the lower part, I make the hollow between the lower lip and the chin.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer details the precise geometric construction of a female head using a coordinate system of vertical and horizontal lines. He provides specific fractional measurements relative to the total body height to locate anatomical features like the eyes, nostrils, and hairline.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, female head, geometry in art, Renaissance anatomy, drafting techniques, facial measurements</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing the proportions of the human head, shifting focus from the male to the female figure.</meta>

Next, I draw a slanted line from the point where the vertical **t** ends, until the horizontal **p** meets the corner **a. y.** Over this line, between **p** and **q**, I draw the forehead; but the nose I draw below that, between **q** and **t**.

Afterward, I divide the second <term>square grid</term> <note>original: "vierung"; the geometric box used to frame the head</note> of the <term>frontal view</term> <note>original: "fursichtigen haubtes"</note> of the head. First, I do this with <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerchlinien"</note> by <term>projecting</term> them <note>original: "vbertrag"; the method of carrying measurement lines from the profile view over to the frontal view</note> from the first grid; then I also divide it with vertical lines. I label the two vertical sides of this grid with **a** and **b**. Then I divide the space between **a** and **b** with nine lines—**d, e, f, g, h, i, k, l, m**—into ten equal fields. Furthermore, I divide the sections **a** to **d** and **m** to **b** with two lines, **c** and **n**, each in the middle. Once the grids in both squares are drawn across each other, within which the parts of the face are contained, I then draw the contour lines. I do this with smaller and gentler features and a softer style than that of a man’s face. First, I draw the head from the side so that the top of the head touches the horizontal **n** at the vertical **j**. Likewise, the back touches the vertical **b** between the horizontals **p** and **q**. Then I draw the front of the head: the forehead, nose, mouth, chin, cheeks, ears, and then the eyes, and the bareness of the forehead, into all the previously indicated marks. And I make the neck 1/14th part thick <note>Dürer uses a fractional system where the neck's thickness is one-fourteenth of the total body height</note>.

Afterward, I draw the contour lines between the cross-lines of the frontal grid. First, the rounding of the head, which touches the horizontal **n** in the middle, and on both sides the verticals **a** and **b** between **p** and **q**. Then I draw the jawlines down from the two verticals under the horizontal **r** to the end of the chin. I draw the ears on both sides between the verticals **a, c** and **n, b**, and between the horizontals **q** and **s**. But the inner <insert>ear folds</insert> <note>original: "zipffellein," referring to the tragus or inner cartilage</note> I draw inside the verticals **c** and **n**. I mention this again so that it may be better noted. Then I draw the neck down from under the ears to the horizontal **y**; there I make it 1/15th part wide. Then I draw the bareness of the forehead on both sides beyond the middle between the verticals **a, c** and **n, b**, between the horizontals **p** and **q**. And from the line **g** on the horizontal **p**, I draw the hair above the forehead on both sides, with the bareness extending upward until the middle between the horizontal lines **o** and **p**. Next, I draw both eyes so that their corners touch the vertical lines **e, g** and **i, l**. I draw the eyebrows by the verticals **f** and **k** over the horizontal **q**, letting them end on both sides under this line, halfway between the verticals **d, e** and **l, m**. Then I draw the nose, but at the bottom, I do not let its <term>nostrils</term> <note>original: "pelle"; the outer wings of the nose</note> touch the two verticals **g** and **i**. However, I draw the mouth so that both its corners touch them.

Next, I draw the back of the head following the outer lines of the frontal view and give it its proper character.

Then I draw the head by projecting it into the <term>plan view</term> <note>original: "nydergedruckten grund"; a top-down or foreshortened projection</note>, just as was described before for the man’s head. I have sketched this so precisely following this that it will be clear to anyone of even limited understanding, even if there were no writing accompanying it.

And if I wish to make this woman’s head taller or wider, I do as I showed before with the man’s head. However, it is not done to the same extent, for it is fitting that a woman’s head remains smaller than a man’s.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer provides detailed geometric instructions for constructing a female head in profile and frontal views using a coordinate grid. He emphasizes that female features should be drawn with softer, gentler lines and slightly smaller proportions compared to the male head.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, human proportion, female head, frontal view, profile view, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance art theory, anatomy, coordinate system</keywords>

**In what follows**, I will describe and draw <note>original: "auffreiſſen," meaning to draft or sketch technically</note> a young child. For this, I will use the same order as with the previously described figures. First, for as long as I want to make the child, I set three upright lines or points that represent them next to each other. With the first, I will make the child from the side; with the second, from the front; and with the third, the child from behind. These three upright lines I close at the top and bottom with two cross-lines <note>horizontal lines</note>; let the upper one be **a** and the lower one **z**.

Furthermore, I divide the length of the parts in this child with cross-lines. Since **a** and **z** are to touch the crown of the head and the soles of the feet, I measure down a 4th part from the crown. There I place a cross-line **b**, which touches the end of the neck at the body in the back and the front, where the pit of the throat <note>the suprasternal notch, the small dip at the base of the neck</note> is located.

From this height, I create a square for the head in profile <note>original: "nebenſichtigenn haupt"</note>, in which I enclose the head. I mark the front upright side where the nose stands out also with an **a**, and the back with a **b**. Before I proceed further with the child, I want first to fully describe the head.

Initially, I divide this according to its length, the height and depth, with cross-lines, as has often been mentioned before. From the cross-line **a** downward, I set a cross-line at a 24th part; this touches the upper forehead in front. But from the **a** downward at a 16th part, I set a cross-line **c**, which goes through...

| Since I have shown the lengths of the parts of the child, I will further describe the thicknesses of the <term>side-profile</term> <note>original: "nebensichtigen"</note> child at all points of the indicated lengths. | Since I have now shown all lengths and thicknesses of the side-view child, I then project from this—by means of transfer—all lengths of the entire division to the position of the <term>front-facing</term> <note>original: "fursichtigen"</note> child, together with all horizontal lines of the head, and I mark the parts of the body with points. |
| This child is 2/15th parts thick at the point of the shoulder joints. | |
| At the height of the chest, 1/6th part. | |
| | And first, I divide the front-facing face with vertical lines that indicate all the widths of the parts. |

Initially, I make the <term>square frame</term> <note>original: "vierung"</note> of the front-facing head 2/9th parts wide, and I label the two vertical sides as **a** and **b**. This square I divide with a vertical line **c** into...

<summary>This page provides highly detailed anatomical measurements for a child's proportions, specifically for the side profile and arms, using a fractional system. It concludes by explaining how to project these measurements to create a front-facing drawing using a grid system.</summary>
<keywords>child proportions, Renaissance anatomy, measurement system, side profile, front view, arm length, baby fat folds, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing the human figure using a grid system, moving from the specific geometry of the face to the proportional widths of the entire body in a frontal view.</meta>

The center line between the two sides goes through the crown, the nose, the mouth, and the chin. After that, I divide the sections a–c and c–b into two equal parts using two lines, d and e; these two lines touch the outer corners of the eyes and the width of the neck.

Next, I divide the space between d and e with two lines, f and g, into three equal fields; these touch the inner corners of the eyes, the outer edges of the nostrils, the corners of the mouth, and the width of the front of the chin.

After that, I divide a–d with h–i and e–b with k–l into six equal fields; these two lines h and l touch both sides of the face's width at the <term>transverse line</term> <note>original: "zwerchen"; referring to a horizontal cross-section of the head</note> marked h. I also draw the lower <term>earlobes</term> <note>original: "orleplein"</note> inside these vertical lines h and l, and I draw the upper part of the ears against the <term>squaring</term> <note>original: "vierung"; the grid or square frame used for the drawing</note> of the vertical lines a and b. The two vertical lines i and k should touch the outer curve of the cheeks.

Next, I divide both sides, a–h and l–b, into five equal fields using four points. From the two points closest to a and b, I draw two vertical lines, m and n; these should touch both sides of the width of the head between the transverse lines e and f. The bare part of the forehead should extend beyond the two vertical lines h and l on both sides between the transverse lines e and f.

I end the eyebrows on both sides in the middle between the vertical lines i and d, and e and k, below the transverse line f.

Now that this <term>frontal</term> <note>original: "fursichtige"; literally "fore-sighted," meaning a front-on view</note> grid is also marked with cross-lines, within which all parts of the face are made, I then use all the points that indicate the length of the parts to determine the widths of the person's body.

| | |
|---|---|
| First, at the height of the <term>shoulder joints</term> <note>original: "achselglieder"</note>, I make the body one-fourth wide. | And the width at the end of the abdomen I make four-fifteenths. |
| And on this line, I place the shoulder joints two-ninths apart from each other. | At the end of the <term>genitals</term> <note>original: "nyllein"</note>, I make the leg one-fifteenth and one-sixteenth wide. |
| | At the end of the buttocks, one-eighth. |
| Next, I make the width at the height of the chest across the shoulders two-sevenths. | At the <term>thigh-curve</term> <note>original: "wünen"; likely referring to the fleshy part of the upper leg</note> of the leg, two-seventeenths. |
| between the <term>armpits</term> <note>original: "ochssen"</note>, one-fifth. | Above the knee, one-nineteenth and one-twentieth. |
| between the <term>nipples</term> <note>original: "tutlein"</note>, one-seventh. | In the middle of the knee, one-twelfth. |
| At the waist, one-fifth. | Below the knee, one-thirteenth. |
| Across the navel, four-seventeenths. | In the middle of the calf, two-twenty-thirds. |

<note>Dürer uses a fractional measurement system where the "Measuring rod" (Meßstab) is 1/6th of the total body height. This rod is further divided into 10 "numbers" (zal), each number into 10 "parts" (teyl), and each part into 3 "small pieces" (trümlein).</note>

But between the forehead and the end of the chin, I divide the space with two lines into three equal fields: in the uppermost I place the forehead; in the second, the nose, eyes, and ears; and in the third, the mouth and chin.

<note>Dürer's measurement system: A 'Meßstab' (measuring rod) represents the total height of the figure. A 'Zahl' (major unit) is 1/6th of the rod. A 'Teil' (part) is 1/10th of a 'Zahl'. A 'Trümlein' (drum) is 1/3rd of a 'Teil'. This allowed for extreme precision in his anatomical drawings.</note>

|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| Behind the elbow — | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| In front of the elbow in width | 0 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| Further toward the hand — | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| At the joint of the hand — | 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |
| And the hand is wide — | 0 | 3 | 4 | 0 |

<note>The "measuring rod" (meßstab), "number" (zall), "part" (teyl), and "tiny piece" (trümlein) refer to Dürer's proportional system where the body's total height is divided into specific fractions for standardized measurement.</note>

Now that all these lengths, thicknesses, and widths have been described, I then draw the figure of the man with its lines correctly therein: by the first upright line from the side, and by the second upright line from the front.

Afterward, I outline the rear-facing man by the third upright line, using the outer lines of the front-facing man, and I make him from behind:

| | measuring rod | number | part | tiny piece |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| Width between the <term>armpits<gloss>original: "ochsen"</gloss></term> — | 1 | 1 | 5 | 9 |
| And from the height of the <term>pit of the throat<gloss>original: "halsgrübleins"</gloss></term> to the rear armpits is — | 0 | 4 | 3 | 0 |
| And the backside is split from the bottom — | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| And the heel is wide — | 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |

<note>The term "ochsen" is an archaic German anatomical term for the armpits (axilla). The "split from the bottom" refers to the division of the buttocks or the start of the legs.</note>

Thus, as described above, I have sketched out this man in the following pages.

Following the man described just before, I will now describe a woman, using the first method of proportion. From the highest point of the head—the crown—downwards:

<note>This page serves as a technical diagram. The figure described in the archival notes is a highly detailed pen-and-ink study. The use of Greek letters (β, ε) and numbers is typical of the period's "Mensur" or measurement systems, which sought to find "divine" mathematical harmony in the human body. The central axis line mentioned in the description acts as the anchor for these measurements, ensuring perfect symmetry from the crown of the head to the heels.</note>

<note>These measurements reflect Dürer’s pioneering work in <term>anthropometry</term> (the scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body). By applying geometric principles to the flesh, Dürer sought to provide artists with a reliable template for creating realistic and harmonious figures.</note>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportions, Renaissance anatomy, mathematical ratios, anthropometry, artistic geometry</keywords>

—two figures of the man and woman have diligently drafted.

If you wish, however, to enlarge the head on these figures, do it thus: increase the vertical line of the figure's length by — 0 | 0 | 8 | 0

And then draw the head from the side view, starting from the forehead, rounding upwards until the predetermined height, and from there backwards and out, and then down to the nape of the neck into the neck.

But for the front-facing head, draw from the assigned height out roundly on both sides and down, and pull the line back in at the transverse line of the <term>eyebrows</term> toward the ears, as I have also indicated in the drafting.

### Posterior View of the Male Figure: Mathematical Proportions

***

**Technical Terms used in this Diagram:**

*   **Anatomy:** The study of the structure of the human body.
*   **Proportion:** The relationship of one part of the body to another in terms of size and scale.
*   **Theory of Proportion:** The systematic study of these relationships, pioneered in the Northern Renaissance by Dürer.
*   **Albrecht Dürer:** The master artist of the German Renaissance who sought to combine art with scientific observation.
*   **Renaissance:** A period of "rebirth" in European history, characterized by a renewed interest in classical learning and the natural world.
*   **Woodcut:** A relief printing technique where a design is carved into a block of wood.
*   **Anthropometry:** The scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body.
*   **Parts and Lines:** The specific units of measurement Dürer used to quantify the human form.

<summary>This page features a technical woodcut illustration of a male figure from behind, showcasing Albrecht Dürer’s rigorous system of anatomical measurements and proportional "Parts and Lines."</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, Renaissance, anthropometry, woodcut, artistic theory, measurement</keywords>

Furthermore, if you stretch this woman out in the shape of a cross with her arms, and raise her hands as high as she is long, and then place a compass with one foot on her navel and the other foot at the soles of her feet and draw a circle all around, the circle's path will touch the furthest parts of the hands and feet. <note>This is a description of the Vitruvian Man principle—that a well-proportioned body fits within both a square and a circle—applied here to the female figure.</note>

Furthermore, I wish to describe another man in the same order as before. Therefore, I first measure the length of the limbs and other necessary things along the first vertical line, namely:

From the highest point of the <term>crown</term> <note>original: "scheytel," the top of the head</note> line to:

Afterward, I divide the space between the forehead and the end of the chin into three equal fields with two <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerchlinien"</note>. In the uppermost, I place the forehead. In the second, the nose, eyes, and ears. In the third, the mouth and chin. I do this with all heads, though for the sake of brevity I will not always mention it.

This page links the side view (lateral) and front view (frontal) proportions.</meta>

<note>Dürer’s measurement system: The "Meßstab" is the total height. This is divided into 6 "Zall" (Numbers/Feet). Each "Zall" is divided into 10 "Teyl" (Parts). Each "Teyl" is divided into 10 "Trümlen" (Minutes/Small Drums).</note>

| Under the kneecap | 0 | 3 | 5 | 0 |
| On the line under the knee | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| Middle of the calf | 0 | 4 | 2 | 0 |

| At the end of the inner calf | 0 | 3 | 5 | 0 |

Now that all lengths, thicknesses, and widths are drafted, I then draw the figure into them with graceful lines. At the first vertical line, I draw it according to the side; but at the second vertical line, I draw its figure facing forward. Afterward, I trace the form of the man facing rearward at the third vertical line using the outer lines of the front-facing man. And I make his buttocks split from the bottom up | 0 | 5 | 5 | 0 |
and I make his heels wide | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

When you stretch out this man's arms and raise his height, you may circumscribe him with a circle <note>original: "zirckelriß" – a line drawn with a compass</note> if you place one foot of the compass in his navel. And as described here above, I have drafted this man entirely according to my intent, so that one may more clearly follow it.

<summary>This page provides exhaustive numerical tables for the thickness and width of the human body from both side and front views. Dürer concludes by explaining how these measurements are used to construct the front, side, and rear figures, including a reference to the Vitruvian principle of the navel as the center of a circle.</summary>

<note>
**Image Description:** A detailed anatomical study of a man analyzing proportions using geometric lines and numerical measurements. A striking circular arc extends from the raised right hand to the heel, demonstrating the integration of the human body into basic geometric shapes (the circle and square) following the Vitruvian model. <note>original: "vitruvianischem Vorbild" refers to Vitruvius, the Roman architect who inspired Leonardo da Vinci's famous "Vitruvian Man" drawing.</note> This drawing is characteristic of the rigorous proportion theory developed during the German Renaissance. This image is an outstanding example of the scientific and artistic exploration of the human body; it holds immense value for both art history and the history of medicine.
</note>

# Study of Human Proportion: Male Figure from the Rear

<note>This woodcut illustration serves as a scientific diagram. Dürer was the first to apply rigorous Renaissance geometry to the human body in Northern Europe. To the modern eye, the background may look messy; this is "bleed-through" or "ghosting," where the ink from the text printed on the other side of this thin paper has soaked through over the last five hundred years.</note>

<term>Part</term> 2, <term>Line</term> <note>Dürer’s system divided the body into "Parts" (Teile) and "Lines" (Linien). A "Part" is usually one-tenth of the total body height, and a "Line" is one-tenth of a "Part." This allowed artists to scale a figure to any size while keeping the proportions perfect.</note>
</gloss>

### Technical Vocabulary
**Albrecht Dürer**: The master artist of the German Renaissance.
**Proportion**: The relationship of the size of one part to another.
**Anatomy**: The study of the structure of the human body.
**Human Body**: The central subject of Dürer's geometric inquiry.
**Renaissance**: The period of "rebirth" in art and science.
**Part / Line**: The specific units of measurement invented by Dürer for his proportional system.
**Measurement**: The act of defining the body through numbers rather than just sight.

<summary>This page features a rear-view anatomical study of a man, using Dürer’s specialized "Parts and Lines" system to define the mathematical ideal of the human form. It represents the intersection of fine art and the burgeoning scientific method of the 16th century.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, Renaissance anatomy, mathematical art, woodcut illustration, measurement systems, 16th-century science</keywords>

# PART % <note>The symbol % here likely serves as a section marker or placeholder in the manuscript.</note>

Following the man I have just described, I will now describe a woman in the same order. I will first measure the length of the limbs and the necessary parts of the body along the first vertical line as follows:

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page serves as a technical breakdown of human anatomy, following the rigorous mathematical systems established by the Northern Renaissance master Albrecht Dürer. It provides the specific measurements used to construct a "perfect" human figure in his influential 1528 treatise.</meta>

->[Anatomical Proportion Diagram]<-

<note>The following values represent the numeric measurements inscribed on the human figure and within the diagrammatic framework. The abbreviation 'T' refers to <term>Teile (Parts)</term>, which were Dürer’s primary unit of measurement, while the smaller numbers often refer to <term>Trümlein (Small Bits)</term>, which were subdivisions used for extreme precision.</note>

Next follows how to measure a thin, narrow man who may be used for a tall person; and I shall also treat him in the order described before, measuring first the length along the first vertical line through all the limbs and other things, namely as follows.

From the crown of the head <note>original: "scheyttellini"; literally the line where the hair parts</note> down to:

| | Scale | Number | Part | Bit |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the top vertebra <note>original: "hindern wirbel"; refers to the atlas or the nape of the neck</note> is — | 0 | 0 | 7 | 0 |
| To the forehead — | 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |
| To the end of the chin — | 0 | 7 | 0 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder flesh — | 0 | 8 | 0 | 0 |

| Under the back armpits — | 0 | 3 | 8 | 0 |
| Upon the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note> — | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |

Now that these lengths, thicknesses, and widths are all described and plotted, I then draw the figure of the man correctly within them using lines. At the first vertical line, I depict him from the side; but at the second, I depict him facing forward. After that, I outline the rear-facing man at the third vertical line using the outer lines of the front-facing man.

| | measuring rod | number | part | tiny fraction |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| | **†** | **z** | **I** | **%** |
| And I make the split of the buttocks from the bottom upwards | 0 | 5 | 8 | 0 |
| And I make the heels wide | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |

And in the same way that I provide this man described above, I have sketched him in what follows, to be adjusted according to the measuring rod. One should also know that every body is wider across its surface from the front than its thickness from the side. However, the legs seen from the side are thicker than they are wide from the front. The figures that were measured with parts and digits in the first booklet can also be measured approximately with the measuring rod. It makes a great difference if a head is taken from one figure and another is set in its place.
