<meta>This page begins the third book of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). It transitions from the standardized proportions of the first two books to the methods for varying and distorting those forms. Catchword "D" refers to the next gathering of the printed book.</meta>

# The Third Book.

In this third book, I will show how one may change and alter the previously described measurements <note>Dürer refers to the "ideal" proportions established in Books I and II</note> according to each person's will, increasing or decreasing everything so that a figure becomes unrecognizable, remaining not at all in its previous shape and measure. I take this up in what follows for the figures of men, women, and children, and likewise in all other things.

But to understand this correctly, one must know above all things what those things are through which such alteration may happen. That can be done through the joining of **opposites** <term>widerwertige: literally 'adverse' or 'contrary' things; Dürer uses this to mean contrasting qualities like large vs. small</term> in all specific measurements or accidents; for in identical things there is no difference except that one is not the other, therefore identical things cannot be distinguished from one another. What, however, may be called opposites useful for measurement are these things following hereafter, such as:

->Large } { Small<-
->Long } or { Short<-
->Wide } { Narrow<-
->Thick } { Thin<-

After that are several attached or accidental things which also alter the shape, such as:

->Young / Old<-
->Fat or Lean<-
->Pleasant / Ugly<-
->Hard / Soft<-

After that, however, one uses other things that serve to change such things, such as:

->Crooked } { Straight <note>original: "schlechte" — in 16th-century German, this meant straight or simple, not "bad" as in modern German.</note><-
->Even } { Uneven<-
->High } or { Low<-
->Round } { Cornered<-
->Pointed } { Blunt<-

These things also alter [the form]:
->{ Upright<-
->{ Transverse<-
->{ Or diagonal<-

It also alters greatly:
->{ Giving<-
->{ Taking<-
->{ Much<-
->{ Little<-

Then one sometimes uses:
->{ Right<-
->{ Left<-
->{ Front<-
->{ Back<-
->{ Top<-
->{ Bottom<-

From such above-mentioned things one can distinguish a thing from the one first made.

<summary>In this introduction to Book III, Dürer explains his theory of variation, arguing that artists can create diverse and unique figures by systematically altering "ideal" proportions using sets of opposites.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, alteration, opposites, artistic theory, human form, measurement, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>
This page continues Dürer’s transition from the first book, which established fixed types of human figures, into the second book, which explores how to mathematically manipulate those figures through "proportionality." The translator has maintained continuity with the terminology of the "thick man" established in the previous book.
</meta>

...to divide the parts differently and make them other than they were before. We wish to put this into practice a little, and first take for ourselves these two words: **Large** or **Small**. These are to be used in all things that one wishes to make large or small. In such a thing, in and of itself, there is no change of form other than that one <unclear>says</unclear> <note>OCR: spricht</unclear></note> this is a large thing or the other is a small thing; yet both can be of one kind and one proportion. Therefore, the written proportions may be made large or small, and each contains within itself its own parts of measure and number. Let us take an example of this in the pattern of a small, scaled-down house, after which a great palace is built and erected; or how a small sphere compares to a large one. Thus the words "large" or "small" remain, and do not change the form, as was said above.

But if the words "large" or "small" are to bring about a distinct change in the form, then this must happen in certain parts and not in the whole at once. However, if one makes some parts larger and others smaller, then the appearance of that same thing becomes different. For example, if you make a figure large above the belt, and smaller from the belt downwards; and again, as mentioned above, using this through the entire <term>limb-measure</term> <note>original: "glidmaß"; referring to the overall anatomical proportions</note> to be used specifically in the smallest as in the largest parts. In this, the variations are infinitely many. Therefore, it is to be noted that no thing is recognized as large or small next to another unless the opposite part is placed against the large or small. Anyone may use this as far as he has need.

Further, we wish to take up the words **Long** or **Short**. From these, we will make the first "thick man" described in the first book both long and short. <note>In Book I, Dürer provided a template for a stout, heavily-muscled male figure.</note> Now note: if this man is lengthened, he becomes thinner and narrower in his measure; but if he is made shorter, he becomes thicker and wider according to his kind and measure. In such lengthening or shortening, one lets the thickness and width remain unchanged in all things, just as they were indicated in the first described man. But if the two men—the lengthened and the shortened—are drawn again in their own measure into the length of the first described man, then their thickness and width all become different from those of the first man from whom they were drawn. And all parts of the length become equal to the first described man again, yet they look unlike one another, because one has become thinner and narrower, and the other thicker and wider. 

Through the method determined above, the length of the limbs is lengthened or shortened comparably to one another between all the <term>transverse lines</term> <note>original: "zwerch linien"; these are the horizontal lines Dürer uses in his diagrams to mark anatomical landmarks like the knees, hips, and shoulders</note> which then indicate the length of the things in the body. If one stretches them far from each other, the image lengthens; if one pushes them close together, it makes the image shorter. Therefore, as spoken above, such things neither give to nor take away from the thickness or width in the entire limb-measure; yet the words "long" or "short" make a distinction in the figures. And if one then measures them anew with the **compass** <note>original: "zirckel"; the primary tool for geometric construction and measurement</note>, their description will be different than that of the first man. Now, it would be a tedious thing if one always had to put in the effort, when lengthening or shortening a figure, to search out every single part with the compass. Therefore, I will hereafter set down a rule for how one shall find this lengthening and shortening equally and easily.

First, I must have a dotted line, after which I will then comparably dot two others, a long one and a short one; this I do thus: I take a line for side **a. b.** as long as the first man of the first book is; this line is dotted by the transverse lines with which the figure is described through all the lengths of the limbs. This line **a. b.**

<summary>Dürer explains the conceptual difference between scaling a figure (large/small) and altering its proportions (long/short). He introduces a geometric method to stretch or compress the "thick man" from Book I while maintaining consistent widths, creating new body types.</summary>
<keywords>proportionality, scale, anatomy, thick man, transverse lines, geometry, compass, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the geometric construction of human figures; this page explains the "transformer" method for scaling proportions.</meta>

<sig>D iiij</sig>

I set [a line] upright before me and place a point **c** at a distance to the side. From this point **c**, I draw straight lines through all the points of the upright line **a-b** and extending further out. Now that **a**, **b**, and **c** have become a triangle and the intersecting lines look almost like a brush, I then place two other parallel upright lines next to the upright **a-b**, one on each side. The shorter one, closer to point **c**, I mark as **d** at the top and **e** at the bottom. The longer one I place further out, marked **f** at the top and **g** at the bottom. The longer I wish to make the man, the further out I place the line; and the shorter the man is to be, the closer I move the line toward point **c**. In this way, whatever we desire is divided by the radiating lines drawn from point **c** through the three upright lines **d-e**, **a-b**, and **f-g**; and each line is divided in exact proportion to the others.

I have accurately drafted the rule as it stands above, not only for this specific use but for wider applications, and I give this figure its own name, calling it a <term>transformer</term> <note>original German: *verkerer*; a geometric tool used to distort or scale proportions</note>, because things are transformed through it. Alongside this transformer, one may use two <term>grids</term> <note>original German: *röstlein*; literally "little grates," referring to the lattice or coordinate grids used by artists to transfer drawings</note>, whether many or few.

Through this <term>transformer</term>, any object that has finite parts can be drawn proportionally into a larger or smaller form, for many wonderful things can be created by it.

Through this <term>transformer</term>, a figure that was previously seven <term>heads tall</term> <note>original German: *haubt lang*; a standard unit of measurement where the total height is calculated by the number of head-lengths</note> can be made eight, nine, or even ten <term>heads tall</term>. If the figure is lengthened by one, two, or three head-lengths between the head and the instep of the foot, then the head must also be lengthened slightly as the situation requires, so that the head does not look too stunted; however, the figure will then no longer be the same number of "heads tall" as it was before.

Special care must be taken when one wishes to use the <term>transformer</term> to make the hands and feet—which were specifically described in the first book—longer or shorter; one must proceed with great diligence.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer describes a geometric device called a "transformer" used to proportionally scale or distort the human figure. He specifically details how to increase a figure's height from seven heads to ten while adjusting the head size to maintain visual harmony.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, transformer, verkerer, scaling, grids, heads tall, artistic theory, Renaissance art</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---

It likely follows a series of lessons on measurement and perspective, possibly from Albrecht Dürer’s foundational 1525 work, 'Underweysung der Messung' (Manual of Measurement). The warning in the transcription indicates that the page appears cluttered due to ink bleeding through from the other side, but only one sentence and the drawing are intended for this page.</meta>

This figure shall be called the <term>reverser</term> <note>original: "verkerer." In early modern geometry and perspective, this refers to a construction that "reverses" or transforms an object's proportions by projecting lines through a single focal point.</note>.

These lines originate from various heights on the left margin and converge sharply to a single point on the right side of the page, which is marked with the letter 'e'. This represents a focal point, illustrating how the eye perceives distance or how light passes through a narrow aperture.]

<meta>catchword: Afterwards</meta>

**S O** now, if these two newly made dotted lines—the long one **f. g.** and the short one **d. e.**—are each drawn through with cross-lines at all the points they have received; then I draw the figure of the man, both long and short, according to the positions and forward-facing views as indicated in the first described booklet, in all his thickness and breadth. And just as this rule shows the length of the elongated and the shortened man, so I have sketched them both as they should be. However, I have not been able to conveniently put the elongated man into print here because of the small size of the paper <note>Dürer often lamented the physical constraints of the printing press and paper formats when trying to illustrate full-scale human proportions.</note>; and therefore I have placed them back into their first-described length and redrawn them according to their newly gained measurements. This is done through the way that follows here, which one may also use at all times for other things. Thus, the measurement on the line **f. g.** becomes the thinner and narrower man, and on the line **d. e.** a thicker, broader man. You may also use this <note>original: "verkerer"</note> <term>transformer</term> <note>A geometric method or device used to distort or scale proportions, allowing one to turn a "standard" figure into one that is taller, shorter, fatter, or thinner.</note> in the same way for the words thick and thin, broad and narrow, as well as for long and short. It is also good and quite fitting that all figures throughout the booklets be drawn in one single length, so that they may be better judged against one another. It is also to be known that through the aforementioned transformer, one may change every single part in the previously made figure and let others remain; yet whatever is taken from one part is added to the other part—this is now said regarding the length of the limbs. But at the same time, the same is to be done on all cross-lines of the figures with which the length is divided, and also between them, to make them thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower. For as often as you have a previously described line and set it up in the transformer, and set two other lines—one before and the other after or behind—it shows what it gives you; and you may put into this transformer whatever you need, and it will change it for you proportionally.

Now I will show how one should act so that one can work from it, and I will make an instrument out of the transformer, so that with it you may find how to make every previously described part longer or shorter, thicker or thinner, broader or narrower, entirely proportional to one another through and through all parts of the entire divided figure's length, thickness, and breadth.

First, draw a cross-line; let the beginning be **a.** and the end **b.** Make this line firm and stationary. But from point **a.** draw another line, the end of which is **c.** The line **a. c.** must be movable; yet the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.** must never move away from each other at point **a.**, but remain fixed together.

And note, when I speak here of points or lines, I do not mean only the points and lines that <term>Mathematics</term> <note>original: "Mathematica". Referring here to Euclidean geometry where points and lines are theoretical, invisible, and have no physical width.</note> provides—although those same invisible, indivisible points and lines are included or intended herein. Rather, I speak here of such points and lines that are dotted and drawn with a needle or a pointed pen against a <term>straightedge</term> <note>original: "richtscheit". A ruler or tool for drawing straight lines.</note>, made understandable to the sight.

And if you now wish to use this instrument, prepare this tool thus: Draw on a flat board suitable for it, crosswise, the aforementioned line **a. b.** and drive a sharp needle into point **a.** stiff and firm; and tie to it a clean, twisted silk thread; stretch it out frontally by the end of **c.** and use it in place of the movable line **a. c.**, for the thread is easily moved back and forth.

When that is thus prepared, then draw from the middle of the cross-line **a. b.** a straight, upright line **d. e.** at right angles upwards.

On this line **d. e.**, the intended part—be it length, thickness, or breadth—which you then wish to <gloss>change</gloss> <note>The gloss "verendñ" is an abbreviation for "verendern" (to change or alter).</note> must be placed so that one end touches the cross-line **a. b.** at the bottom. Afterwards...

<summary>Dürer explains how to use a geometric "transformer" to scale human proportions and provides instructions for building a physical instrument using a board, a needle, and a silk thread to perform these calculations.</summary>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of geometric proportions and measurement, likely from Albrecht Dürer's "Manual of Measurement" (Underweysung der Messung). It introduces a specific proportional scaling tool.</meta>

Take the thread that stands in for line a.c. and stretch it out so that it touches the top of the part you have placed on line d.e. Now lengthen your intended part as much as you want, and set this same lengthened line with one end on the horizontal line a.b. <note>original: "zwerch lini"; a line running across or horizontally</note> at the same angles as line d.e., toward end b. Move it upright until its top touches the stretched thread a.c.; let this vertical line be f.g. 

If you wish to have the part shorter than the one you placed on line d.e., then set your intended shorter line upright at the same angles on the horizontal line a.b., behind the vertical line d.e. toward point a. Move it along line a.b. upright until its top touches the stretched thread a.c.; let this vertical line be h.i. Thus, in this instrument, there are three parallel upright lines next to one another. On line d.e., the first described length is placed; on line f.g., the lengthened part is taken; and from line h.i., the shortened part. Therefore, whatever part is placed on line d.e., the stretched thread a.c.—as it touches the top of the length on line d.e.—measures out corresponding longer or shorter things on both vertical lines f.g. and h.i. This allows one to change almost anything with great ease. 

Much can be achieved with this tool, for whether you move the thread a.c. up or down, it creates proportional parts relative to one another, whether in long, short, or medium things. This remains true even if the parts cannot be easily named by a number that one wishes to measure—such as when one cannot say it is a 1/2 part, a 1/3 part, a 1/5, or a 1/6 part, and so on, as was said before.

I have drawn my aforementioned design below so that everyone can follow it properly and see it before their eyes. For this reason, I call this instrument the <term>Weler</term> <note>Derived from the German "wählen," meaning "to choose" or "to select." This tool acts as a "selector" for proportions.</note> so that it may be recognized, because from it one may choose [weler] whether they want a proportion of much or little. Using this "Weler," the thinner and thicker figures were later made and drawn, as you can see from the diagram of the "Weler." 

In this process of transformation, it should be noted that for every object made this way, the more it is moved into longer, thicker, wider, and larger parts, the further apart all things in the measurement will move from each other. Conversely, the smaller the parts are taken, the closer the things run together. Therefore, the thinner image produced by the <term>Weler</term> is much more delicate than the enlarged image. This method described above can also be done using a quadrant, as indicated by the compass markings <note>original: "zirkelriß"; lines or arcs drawn with a compass</note> in the <term>Weler</term>.

->Let this instrument be called the Weler.<-

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical instructions on the mathematical transformation of human proportions, specifically how to adjust a figure that has been elongated or compressed using his geometric instruments.</meta>

<sig>D iij</sig>

To this elongated man, one fourth of his own length is to be added. And if one wishes to use him, his head must be made a little shorter, because the elongation gives it a misshapen appearance <note>original: "ungestalt"; Dürer warns that purely mathematical stretching can result in a head that looks unnatural or monstrous to the eye.</note>. Therefore, move the chin upward and re-divide the face; or, take the head off at the top and re-divide the face through the <term>transfer-scale</term> <note>original: "weier." This refers to a proportional divider or a specific scale Dürer designed to mechanically shift measurements from one ratio to another.</note>. I have fashioned it here so that it touches the top of the height, making the length from the highest point of the crown to the end of the chin two 15th parts, and from the end of the chin upward to the end of the forehead a 10th part.

Afterward, I divide the face lengthwise again using the transfer-scale; however, it retains the thinness and narrowness it acquired through the scale, just as I have <term>sketched</term> <note>original: "auffgerissen"; Dürer uses this to mean a technical drawing or a draft.</note> such faces next to the figures according to my judgment. I also make the foot two 13th parts long, as it became too short through the transfer-scale; I also make the instep lower as is appropriate. Especially for the sake of <term>beauty</term> <note>original: "wolstandes"; literally 'well-standing,' referring to aesthetic harmony and graceful appearance.</note>, the thighs should be drawn a little thinner and narrower.

In which parts this occurs, I have indicated in the following sketches where I mean it; and this man is one 5th part thinner than the first man described in the first booklet, from whom he was modified. How the head and everything else I mean should be drawn, I have sketched in the following pages so that you may presently see the difference I am proposing between the first described man and the other man derived from him.

It is further to be noted how this shorter man was made; understand it thus: In the <term>transformer</term> <note>original: "verkerer"; a geometric construction or device Dürer used to mechanically invert or alter proportions.</note> where I placed and took the shorter line **d. e.**, I drew two straight lines from both ends of the elongated line **f. g.**, crossing through the middle of the upright line **a. b.** Where their ends intersect the two lines **e. f.** and **c. g.**, the straight line **d. e.** previously mentioned is drawn between them.

When this shortened man is pulled back to his original length, he becomes very thick, as the transfer-scale dictates. (However, one should take away from the thickness and breadth of the head if one wishes, so that it remains human-looking if one intends to use such a figure; and make the foot a 6th part long again.) Such things are sketched hereafter. All this is left to your choice, to make them longer or shorter as you wish.

<summary>Dürer explains how to manually correct the aesthetic distortions—such as a misshapen head or overly short feet—that occur when using geometric tools to stretch or compress the human figure. He emphasizes that while the math provides a base, the artist must adjust the proportions to ensure the figure remains 'human' and beautiful.</summary>

--- Page 200 ---
From the words "Long" and "Short," I wish to demonstrate again how the man's head—which was divided and described by squared cross-lines within its specific cubic <term>squaring</term> <note>original: "vierung"</note> in the first booklet—can be understood as being contained within a <term>cube</term> <note>original: "cubo" or "würffel"</note>. I will show how one may stretch this cube (that is, heighten it) or compress it (press it down). In doing so, one must know how much thinner and narrower it becomes when stretched, or how much thicker and wider it becomes when compressed, in order to maintain equal volume <note>original: "eins gleychen inhalts wegen"; Dürer is explaining how to change the dimensions of a shape while keeping its total mass or volume constant.</note>. Regarding this measure and weight, one must proceed as follows. I take for you a correct, squared cube, equal on all sides, which is as high as the aforementioned man's head; for the head must be contained within this cube. Now, I must first deal with the cube alone before anything is described or drawn within it. Now, let it be according to each person's will how much they wish to heighten, stretch, or compress the cube, and so I begin.

Take one side of the correct cube; I label the bottom **a** and the top **b**. I lengthen this line **a. b.** by an end point **c**, according to how much higher I want the cube to be than it was before. After that, I take the side **a. b.** and the lengthened line **a. c.** and form a right-angled <term>set square</term> <note>original: "winckelhacken", a tool used by craftsmen to ensure 90-degree angles</note> from them, so that **a. c.** is the <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> and **a. b.** is upright, with **a** being the corner. Then, the horizontal line **a. c.** is extended out past the corner **a**; let the end point be **d**. Thus, **d. a. c.** becomes a stretched horizontal line. After that, a <term>diagonal line</term> <note>original: "ort lini"</note> **b. c.** is drawn.

Then I take a correct set square—whether it be made of brass or wood—and lay one side of it against the diagonal line **b. c.** so that the corner of the set square touches point **b**. Then, I draw a straight line at a right angle down from point **b** along the set square. Where this line, drawn at a right angle, intersects the horizontal line **d. a. c.**, I place a point **e**. Once this is done, a comparable line has been found between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**; that is the line **a. e.**

Now you must find another <term>middle line</term> <note>original: "mittel lini"; in this geometric context, Dürer is seeking a mean proportional to calculate the new width of the cube.</note> between **a. b.** and **a. e.** Do it like this: Take the two lines **a. b.** and **a. e.** and set them together to make one stretched horizontal line **e. a. b.** Then draw a stretched upright line upwards from point **a** at equal angles. Then take a <term>compass</term> <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and place one foot in the middle of this joined line **e. a. b.**, and place the other foot on point **e**. Draw a circle upwards from there until it reaches point **b**. Where this circle-line intersects the upright line drawn upwards from point **a**, place an **f**. This is the width of one side of the heightened cube; thus **a. c.** is its height and **a. f.** is its width.

If, however, you want to have the cube lower than its side **a. b.** is, it must become wider. This is done in the same way as before, but in reverse. Just as the side of the cube **a. b.** was previously heightened with point **c**, it must now be shortened with that same point **c**. To find this widening, proceed as follows: Take the side of the first cube **a. b.** and stand it upright before you. As mentioned before, join the shorter line **a. c.** to it at right angles horizontally. Then extend the horizontal line **c. a.** past point **a**; let the end be **d**. Draw the diagonal line **b. c.** and take a set square again. Lay one side of it against the diagonal line **b. c.** so that the corner of the set square touches point **b**. Draw a line at a right angle from point **b** along the set square through the horizontal line **d. a. c.** Where this line is intersected, place a point **e**. Thus the first middle line is found between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**, which is the line **a. e.**

<summary>This page describes a geometric method for altering the proportions of a cube (and thus a human head) by stretching or compressing it while maintaining a consistent volume. Albrecht Dürer explains the use of a set square and compass to find the "mean proportional" lines that determine the new height and width.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, human proportion, cube transformation, Albrecht Dürer, mean proportional, architectural drawing tools, volume conservation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for constructing the proportions of the human head, specifically focusing on how to derive the side lengths of various "cubes" (rectangular prisms) used to frame the face in different perspectives and distortions.</meta>

P ij <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

Now you must find the second middle line between **a. b.** and **a. e.** To do this, proceed as follows: Take the two lines **a. b.** and **a. e.** and join them together end-to-end along their length; this line is **e. a. b.** Then draw an upright line from point **a** at right angles straight upwards. Take a compass and place one foot in the center of this horizontal line **e. a. b.** and with the other foot draw a circular arc from point **e** over the top to point **b**. Where this circular arc intersects the upright line that was drawn upwards from point **a**, mark that point **f**. This line **a. f.** is the length of one side of the expanded, flattened <term>cube</term> <note>In this context, "cube" (cubus) refers to the rectangular box or "bounding box" used to contain the head's proportions.</note>. Its height is then **a. c.**, just as it is accurately drawn hereafter in the figures.

It is also noteworthy that in every cube—whether elongated or flattened—the shape of the face becomes long or short when it is drawn and divided by the crosswise lines.

It should also be noted that if you wish to draw the front-facing <note>original: "fürſichtig" — meaning the frontal view, which often requires adjustment for perspective</note> face within its cube, it should be made narrower by a fifth part of its width, as the measurement dictates. Or do it this way: Take the width of the elongated and flattened cubes, and place eight of those same widths side-by-side. Divide these eight parts into ten equal parts; from one of these ten parts, establish the width of the cube. For the front-facing face, once the narrowing has been done in the long and short cubes, then divide the lines again using the <term>transfer grid</term> <note>original: "weier" — a specialized proportional scale or grid used by Dürer to transfer and adjust measurements between different views</note> and draw the features of the face into it. This method described above for the head is to be used for the entire body.

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's geometric study of human proportions from his "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). Having previously established a "standard" head, he now demonstrates how to manipulate that form using a coordinate grid system.</meta>

**E**s is possible to further change the <term>cube<note>original: "cubo." Dürer uses this term to describe the three-dimensional geometric grid or "cage" that contains the proportions of the head.</note></term> (whereby the head takes on a different shape) in the following manner, as follows hereafter: so that the <term>cube</term> is expanded equally on all sides on the uppermost <term>plane<note>original: "plano." A flat geometric surface used as a reference point in his coordinate system.</note></term> or level; and by as much as it is slanted outwards at the top, it is drawn in and contracted by an equal amount on the bottom <term>plane</term>.

Afterwards, one does the opposite of this. Now that the <term>cube</term> has been distorted in these two ways—the first time wide at the top and narrow at the bottom, and conversely narrow at the top and wide at the bottom—I then draw the straight lines of the head previously described <note>Dürer refers to the "standard" or ideal head proportions established in the earlier sections of the book.</note> back into the transformed grid, along with the contour lines of the face. In this way, two types of faces are produced: the first head becomes large at the top and small at the bottom, but the other head becomes small at the top and large at the bottom. This method may be used for the entire body.

And just as the distortion in the <term>cube</term> is applied at the top and bottom, one should also apply it in the <term>cube</term> from back to front; for if the <term>cube</term> is expanded on its front <term>plane</term>, it is contracted by that same amount on its back <term>plane</term>. The same applies if one does the opposite.

<summary>Dürer describes a method of "topological transformation," explaining how to distort the human head by widening or tapering the geometric grid (the "cube") in which it is drawn. This allows an artist to systematically create different facial types, such as those with broad foreheads and narrow chins or vice versa.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, coordinate grid, facial distortion, Renaissance art theory, anthropometry, mathematical drawing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. Having established the "standard" measurements of the human head in previous sections, Dürer here explains how to mathematically distort those proportions using geometric grids (cubes and rhombuses).</meta>

<sig>P iiij</sig>

<warning>Slight foxing and water staining on the right-hand margin.</warning>

and then, if the stretched lines, together with the shape of the face, are drawn back proportionally using the transfer-selector <note>original: "weler," a tool or method Dürer used for mapping points from one grid to another</note>, the face in the first cube <term>cube: here used as a three-dimensional geometric frame for a drawing</term> becomes large at the front and small at the back; and conversely, the face in the second cube becomes small at the front and the head large at the back, just as I have also drawn out <note>original: "auffgerißen," a technical term for architectural or geometric drafting</note> hereafter.

One may also, in such a transformation <note>original: "verkerung," meaning a geometric distortion or reversal</note>, let the plane of the cube at the top or bottom run level rather than diagonally, as is also shown in the drawing hereafter; and such methods may be used in many ways.

Yet another transformation to use with the cube is to make the two sides into a rhombus shape <note>original: "rauten weyß," meaning diamond-shaped</note>. This occurs when two opposite diagonal angles are made narrow, while the other two diagonal angles become wide. From this, you can first place one of the two narrow angles at the front by the crown of the head, and the other at the back by the neck; thus, a wide angle remains at the back of the head and the other at the front by the chin. One also does the reverse of this, as is also drawn out hereafter. The first head gains a pointed forehead, but the second a high head at the back. These heads do not retain the same volume <note>original: "innhalt"</note> compared to the first head described in the first book.

Every object drawn within a cube is transformed just as often as the cube itself is transformed, provided that the same object is proportionally re-divided by the transformer or transfer-selector.

<insert>
**Th**ese next transformed faces are distorted along with the entire <term>cube-grid</term> <note>original: "cubo"; Dürer uses a 3D coordinate system projected onto a 2D plane</note>; however, everything is proportionally redistributed from the first described head by the <term>transformer</term> <note>original: "verkerer"</note> or <term>transferrer</term> <note>original: "weier"</note> and yet remains changed. And for that reason, I will proceed further with the first described face, but leaving its main body as it was before. But the division of the features in the face I wish to change through the shifting of the <term>horizontal</term> <note>original: "zwerch"</note>, vertical, and <term>diagonal</term> <note>original: "ort"</note> lines. By this, I mean to show that one may use this same method with the previously transformed faces in the <term>cube-grid</term>, and how so many various shapes of faces may be made. Such ways and means are infinitely many; therefore, different shapes of people may be made always and as often as one desires. Now we will take up again the first described face of the first booklet, and in it deal with the <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> according to the side-view, through which all things are distinguished according to their length. And principally there are three of these that divide the largest parts. Therefore, it is to be noted that the top line *k.* first touches the top of the forehead; the second line *l.* goes through the <term>eyebrows</term> <note>original: "augpraen"</note> and touches the <term>ears</term> <note>original: "orn"</note>; and the line *m.* indicates the end of the nose. If one now shifts these lines up or down, away from or toward each other, they create high or low fields in between; for what the horizontal lines take from one field, they give to the other. Through such shifting, you may make shorter or longer whichever part of the face you wish. And in the same way that you may shift these three specific lines *k. l. m.* back and forth, you may also shift the other horizontal lines *s. r. q. p. o.* between them, so that they remain straight lines; or you may shift them a little diagonally, slanted or crooked, and draw them contrary to one another, whereby the fields in between become wide in one part and narrow in the other. Thus you may shift everything that can be made long, short, wide, narrow, thick or thin, equal or unequal according to your will. For every horizontal line can be shifted specifically in three ways: upwards, downwards, or diagonally. Also, all horizontal lines may be shifted upwards or downwards at once, or half of them upwards and the other half downwards, or arbitrarily however one wishes. The same is to be done with the vertical lines. And whatever is changed and should be drawn proportionally again from the first face into a <term>transformed</term> <note>original: "verkertß"</note> one, that must happen through the <term>transformer</term> or <term>transferrer</term>. Therefore, whoever seeks in these things will find strange and wonderful things. And according to the shape of the face you intend to have, accordingly direct the straight lines which shall provide such change.
</insert>

For as said before, when the <term>horizontal lines</term> are shifted close together, whatever is made therein becomes short; but when they are shifted far from one another, whatever is made between them becomes long. But such things occur between the vertical...

Now I will put some of the aforementioned into practice. This seems to me to create a good form, because the shape of the face is thereby evenly divided. Therefore, I have sketched such a transformed face below with its lines from the side and front, for you to follow accordingly.

Furthermore, it should be noted that heads sometimes have long or short faces; this is to be understood as follows. If the face in the head is very long, then the head—as far as it is covered with hair above the forehead—becomes very low. Therefore, I will set such a long face so that line **k** is placed upon line **i**. After that, the other lines **o, l, p, q, m, r, s** should be proportionally divided again between **k** and **n** using the divider. Thus, the hair growth at the top is entirely removed.

If, however, one intends to make the face short, the head itself gains a high, pointed top. Therefore, next to the previously made head with the long face, I will place and sketch one with a short face in its form, as I describe hereafter. Take the forehead line **k**, move it down a third of the way between **i** and **n**, and then divide the other transverse lines **o, l, p, q, m, r, s** proportionally again using the divider, just as it is presented before your eyes.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying the proportions of the human head through geometric manipulation, a method Albrecht Dürer developed to create diverse facial types from a single ideal model.</meta>

Nevertheless, such a transformation exists where the **squared frame** <term>quadrate body<note>original: gefiert corpus; a square grid used as a reference frame for the head</note></term> in which the head is described is made narrow in the front and wide in the back; and as much as is taken from the front, so much is added to the back.

One does the same in the opposite way; if one then draws all the features proportionally anew into this distorted squared frame as is proper, two faces result from it, just as I have drafted both in the two floor plans <note>original: grunden; here referring to the base grid or plan</note> printed below.

Now note further that all the **horizontal lines** <term>transverse lines<note>original: zwerch linien</note></term> in the head are to be shifted by the **distorter** <term>falsifier<note>original: felscher; a geometric method or tool used to "falsify" or distort regular proportions</note></term> which will be described hereafter, so that the fields between them lengthen upwards and become shorter below. One also does this in reverse, or in one half of the head—either top or bottom—or between whichever horizontal lines one chooses.

This also brings about great differences in the shifting <note>original: verruckung</note> of the lines in the opposite manner; if you now shift a line downwards which you previously shifted upwards. This must also be observed in the **vertical lines**, shifting them forwards or backwards. Therefore, I will further describe several faces to show how my aforementioned theory should be put into practice; I expect this to provide cause for much further thought on what might yet be created beyond what is shown here. However, I will use certain shifts here as follows below.

<summary>Dürer explains how to distort a standard head profile by manipulating its underlying grid, stretching or compressing horizontal and vertical lines to create various facial shapes.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, head proportions, grid distortion, facial types, drafting techniques, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
First, it is our intention to completely alter the face we previously described by moving the horizontal lines, so that nothing remains in its original place. We may wish to create a long or short forehead, a long or short nose, or a long or short chin. If it happens that you move the horizontal line **l** significantly upwards toward line **k**, a short forehead will result between them, and the nose will be lengthened between **l** and **m**. However, if you move line **l** downwards toward line **m**, you will have a long forehead between **k** and **l** and a short nose between **l** and **m**. But if you let line **l** stand and move the horizontal line **m** upwards, a short nose will again be created between **l** and **m**, but the mouth and chin will become long. If line **m** is moved downwards, there will be a long nose between **l** and **m** and a short mouth and chin.

Now, the two lines **k** and **m** can be moved far apart from each other, so that line **k** is near line **i** and line **m** is near line **n**. In this case, the height of the head and the length of the mouth and chin remain short, but the forehead and nose become very long. If, however, you move the two horizontal lines **k** and **m** close together, the height of the head becomes long, as do the mouth and chin, but the forehead and nose become very short. If you move **k** and **l** high upwards, you will get a short head and a short forehead. After that, divide the other horizontal lines between **l** and **n** equally again using the <term>transformer</term> <note>original: "verkerer"; likely a mathematical instrument or a geometric method for reversing or distorting proportions</note>, and you will get a long nose, mouth, and chin. You may do the exact opposite of this, and you will get a high head and long forehead, with a short nose, mouth, and chin. 

But here is another shift: move line **k** near to line **i**, and line **m** near to line **l**, and let line **l** stand. From this results a low head, a long forehead, a short nose, and a long lip and chin. Do the opposite of this: move line **k** near to line **l**, and line **m** near to line **n**; thus results a high head, a short forehead, a long nose, and a short mouth and chin. In such matters there would be much to demonstrate, which is omitted here for the sake of brevity. It should also be noted in this "giving and taking" <note>an idiomatic way of describing the adjustment of proportions</note> that one should move the lines in such a way that one does not strain nature too much, so that the result remains human. 

Anyone who wishes to put such things into practice can do with the other horizontal lines **o, p, q, r, s** just as you have done with the three lines **k, l, m**. Thus, according to our will, the parts are divided to be made long or short through the horizontal lines as we intend. And if things are changed in the first assumed <term>square</term> <note>original: "vierung"; the basic grid used for drafting proportions</note>, even if only using straight lines, one may draw the points at the top forward and upward, or at the back downward, and vice versa; or leave some parts straight and make others curved. One may also shift the previously described <term>diagonal lines</term> <note>original: "ort linien"; lines defining the corners or angular boundaries of the head</note> of the first head near the nose however one wishes. One can also "break" the straight lines, either upwards or downwards, and in the vertical lines, forwards or backwards. The same is done with the diagonal lines. In some parts, you may turn the lines wherever you wish; you may also slide the fields between the horizontal lines—in which the lines are broken—forwards or backwards, upwards or downwards. Everyone will find in practice what I am talking about.

So that you understand what I call a "broken line," <note>original: "gebrochne lini"</note> mark my words: let a given line be **a b**. Place a point **c** anywhere you wish on it. At that point, break the line apart; move one half upwards or downwards. Or, set two points on the aforementioned line, such as **c** and **d**. Break that segment **c d** out and move it upwards or downwards. In the same way, vertical lines are to be broken forwards or backwards, and likewise with diagonal lines. Therefore, I will explain how one uses broken diagonal lines in changing the face. But first, look below at the diagram of the broken line mentioned above, and then at the drawings of the distorted faces described just before.

<summary>This page illustrates how to create diverse facial profiles by systematically shifting the horizontal lines of a proportional grid. Albrecht Dürer demonstrates that by mathematically altering the spacing between the brow, nose, and chin, an artist can depict a wide variety of human characters.</summary>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. Having established the "ideal" or standard measurements for a head in previous sections, he now explains how an artist can manipulate those geometric lines to create a variety of different facial types and expressions.</meta>

**AND** as was previously indicated in the first described head of the first book—where the **horizontal** and **upright** lines were drawn as level lines—these lines are changed again if one **breaks** <note>Dürer uses "break" (brechen) to mean bending a straight line into an angle or a curve.</note> them, and also if one makes **diagonal** <term>diagonal (ober ort)</term> <note>Literally "over the point/corner," Dürer uses this term to describe shifting a line so it remains straight but is no longer perfectly vertical or horizontal.</note> lines out of them. Therefore, the two terms "broken" and "diagonal" must always be kept in mind wherever they are needed.

Now, I will first use the term **diagonal** for all horizontal and upright lines: I either shift each horizontal line at the back or the front, upward or downward. Similarly, I also shift the upright lines, either at the top or the bottom, forward or backward. Through this, however, they all remain straight. For that reason, you must also **bend** each of the previously mentioned lines individually into an arc or a snake-like wave—upward or downward, forward or backward—and **break** each line where it is necessary for your use. 

We shall begin with the **forehead line**, drawing it upward or downward at the back or the front. Likewise, the line of the **eyebrows** should be "broken" at their location for as long as the eyebrows are, and drawn either upward or downward at both ends; or broken in the middle above the eyes, with the "break" pulled upward or downward. For you may curve the eyes upward or downward according to each person's specific traits. This is also to be done with the **corners of the eyes**: if the length of the eye is broken out of the line, you may turn it with the front or back corners upward or downward, just as a **needle in a compass** <note>original: "zünglein in ein compast." Dürer compares the tilting of the eye's axis to the pivoting needle of a magnetic compass.</note> is turned. You may also make the inner or outer corners of the eyes deeper or shallower.

Likewise, if the **nose line** is broken, it may be drawn with its back tips upward or downward. The same is to be done with the **mouth**, and similarly with the **jawbone**, which may be pulled upward or downward at the back. It is also very distinct to see from the side how the **neck** is placed forward on the head near the chin; and conversely, shifted diagonally far back from the chin. In the latter case, the head becomes quite upright, but in the former, it inclines toward the earth. 

Furthermore, we have the **ears** to shift within their square frame: upward, downward, forward, backward, or diagonally, on whichever side you wish. You may also make the square of the ear long, short, wide, or narrow; large or small. Some ears lie flat against the head, while others protrude far out. 

From these instructions, all straight things can be drawn diagonally, curved, slanted, or angled. From this, you will find how you can make all heights lower, thicknesses thinner, and flat areas slanted. If the shape of the face is then drawn back in between such pre-determined altered lines, the appearance of the previous shape will be entirely changed. In all of this, one must take very specific note of the terms of distinction described at the beginning. For from this, one finds that there are **three types of faces** to be seen from the side: namely, a **flat, level face**; a **convex** <note>original: "außgebogens"</note> face, like the **heads of hares**; and a **concave** <note>original: "eynbogens"</note> face. Such inward or outward bending may be drawn through curved, straight, or broken lines.

<margin>·</margin> In this, one must use "give and take" <note>An artistic principle of compensation—adjusting one part to balance another.</note>. These three types of faces can be varied infinitely. One may also make these three types of faces sharp and highly prominent, or flat. But to demonstrate my writing, I have sketched my meaning of such three faces hereafter.

<summary>Dürer explains how to vary facial features by "breaking" (bending) or "tilting" (diagonalizing) the standard grid lines of the head, leading to three profile types: flat, convex, and concave.</summary>
<keywords>geometry in art, facial types, profile, Albrecht Dürer, distortion techniques, convex and concave, 16th-century art theory</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s exploration of facial types, moving from simple curves to the geometric transformation of the entire proportional grid.</meta>

### A level face. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; An outward-curved face. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; And an inward-curved face.

<note>The image shows three human profiles inscribed within proportional grids, demonstrating different facial curvatures.</note>

---

There are yet two other types of faces to be looked at from the side, which are to be shifted within their <term>grid</term> <note>original: "Bierung," literally "squaring"; Dürer's term for the proportional coordinate system</note> in this manner. The two <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> of the grid—those at the bottom and the top—remain straight horizontal lines in both versions. However, both grids must be shifted <term>rhombus-wise</term> <note>original: "rautens weiß"; skewed into a diamond shape</note>. Understand it like this: the first grid is pulled forward by its upper front corner and pulled backward by its lower back corner. One does the opposite for the second grid. After that, one divides all the previously described parts again using the straight horizontal lines. But when you divide the <term>vertical lines</term> <note>original: "auffrechten"</note> back in, they—along with both sides—become purely slanted lines, which lean forward in the first instance and lean backward in the second. Then, draw the contour lines of the face into the grid again; thus you will find what becomes of them in both grids, just as I have <term>sketched out</term> <note>original: "auffgerissen"; a technical term for drafting or architectural drawing</note> these faces immediately following the three previously mentioned ones.

### A face leaning forward. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; A face leaning backward.

<note>The lower diagrams illustrate the deformation of the facial profile when the coordinate grid is skewed into a rhombus shape, resulting in forward-leaning or backward-leaning profiles.</note>

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains how to mathematically distort a facial profile by skewing its proportional grid into a rhombus, resulting in faces that appear to lean forward or backward.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry in art, facial profile, coordinate transformation, Renaissance drawing, anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's exploration of "shifting" or distorting a geometric grid to create a vast variety of human facial types. It follows the previous discussion on horizontal distortions by introducing vertical shifts and the concept of the "oblique line" (ort lini) to define the profile.</meta>

<sig>Q iij</sig>

In these shifted faces, all **vertical** and **horizontal** lines may also be moved, just as was permitted in the previous examples. Furthermore, instruction is needed on how one may work with the vertical lines to differentiate the shapes of faces. Given that the two sides of the square framing the side-view of the face are the front vertical **a** and the rear **b**, and the six other lines between them—**c. d. e. f. g. h.**—divide it into seven equal fields: these may be shifted according to anyone's will, just like the horizontal lines. Each individual field between the vertical lines may be moved specifically, either backward or forward, as can several of them together or all of them at once.

And in the same way as was shown before regarding making things long or short with horizontal lines, so it is with vertical lines to make things **thick, thin, wide,** or **narrow**; here, one must simply "give and take" <note>This "give and take" refers to the principle of compensation: if you add width to one area, you must subtract it from another to maintain the geometric relationship.</note>. And as I do with straight lines, so one also does with curved, sloping, and <term>diagonal lines</term> <note>original: "ort linien." Dürer uses this term for lines that run at an angle, often determining the slope of the profile from forehead to chin.</note>. And if one wishes to use such methods, one must pay attention. First, on the forehead: whatever one takes away from its protrusion should be added to the height of the nose. This is also done in reverse. This may happen through lines that are bent outward, bent inward, or straight but angled. This should be done in all parts throughout the entire face when viewed from the side.

Therefore, opposing elements in these transformations must always be placed together. This happens based on what was said above: if you push one part forward, you then push the other part above or below it backward. This then makes a part thick or wide, thin or narrow.

Now, if the change is made in the whole head as a unit—between the horizontal lines and by means of the vertical, upper diagonal, or angled lines—one must also note that in every individual small part of the face, the terms "even," "outward-curved," and "inward-curved" <note>original: "eben oder auß vñ eynbogen." Dürer is describing convex and concave surfaces.</note> are to be used. This applies just as well to the parts as to the three different facial types. From this, it follows that you may make a flat, an outward-curving, or an inward-curving forehead; the same is to be done with the nose, lips, mouth, and chin, and in all other things.

It is to be noted that the line by which the roundness of the head is made, when viewed from the side, may be drawn in many ways. For one person has a flat head, another a pointed one, a third an angular one, and so on through many varieties, just as nature provides many kinds.

Now we wish to speak further of the <term>diagonal line</term> <note>original: "ort lini"</note> which is drawn because of the nose. This may be leaned backward until it reaches the top of vertical line **d**; it may also be drawn vertically in the middle at **a** or **c**; it may also be leaned forward at the top toward vertical line **a**. But in such things, I consider the "proportional" things <note>Dürer uses "vergleychlichen ding" to mean features that are balanced or harmonious.</note> to be the most beautiful. Although the other extreme things <note>original: "abgeſchidnen ding," meaning "deviating" or "departed" from the norm.</note> may bring wonder, they are nonetheless not all lovely. Therefore, an example is to be taken from the aforementioned indicators: one might say no pointed head, nor any flat head, is well-formed; rather, a round head is considered handsome, because it is a middle ground between the others. This does not prove that every middle ground between all things is the best; I only suggest this to be used in certain things. As when one says: "that is too long or too short a face," and likewise in its parts, such as "that is a forehead too long, short, bulging, or hollowed-out." The same is to be done with the nose; for some have large, hooked, long, and overhanging noses. Conversely, some have very short or snub-noses, upturned, thick, or bulbous...

<summary>Dürer explains how to manipulate a vertical grid to alter facial width and profile slopes, emphasizing that while geometric extremes create "wondrous" variety, a moderate "middle" shape is most aesthetically pleasing.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportion, vertical lines, oblique lines, profile geometry, aesthetics of the middle ground, nose shapes, head shapes</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of facial variations and the geometric grid system used to depict them, following previous descriptions of head shapes. The focus here shifts to specific features like eyes, mouths, and chins, and how to manipulate the underlying proportional lines.</meta>

<i>

...set deep in between the eyes, or they are set high, level with the forehead. Furthermore, some have deep, small eyes, or high, large, bulging eyes; some open their eyes narrowly like a pig, and sometimes pull their lower lid further up than the upper lid is pulled down. There are also some who tear their eyes open as wide as a circle, so that one can see the entire eyeball. Then there are some whose eyebrows are raised high above the eyes; for others, they lie right upon them or hang over them; and some eyebrows are thin, while others are thick.

Furthermore, some have thick, high, club-shaped <note>German: *kolbete*—referring to a bulbous or heavy, rounded shape</note> mouths, or thin, "bitten-in" lips; for some, the upper lip protrudes beyond the lower, and vice versa, and often one is thicker than the other. Some have a long space between the nose and the lip, others a short one. Likewise, many a man has a thick, broad, large chin, another a small, pointed one; for some, the chin is ground away toward the neck, while for others it protrudes far from the neck; some are long and others short, as was mentioned above, for these are determined by the <term>transverse lines</term> <note>German: *zwerch linien*—horizontal lines used in Dürer’s grid system to mark features like the mouth or brow</note>.

Furthermore, some faces are "stepped" <note>German: *ſtafflecht*—referring to the profile angle or slant of the face</note>, by which it is understood that they protrude furthest at the top and then recede more and more as they go down. It is also the opposite: protruding at the bottom and receding more and more as they go up. For this, <term>broken lines</term> <note>German: *gebrochen linien*—lines that change angle or "break" rather than running straight, used to map the slant of a profile</note> are to be used. Much could be written about these things and how they should be applied to the very smallest parts, but a diligent seeker will soon find this for himself.

It is also granted here that one may create more or fewer of the previously described <term>vertical</term>, <term>transverse</term>, and <term>diagonal lines</term> <note>German: *auffrechten*, *zwerchen*, and *ort linien*—the three axes of Dürer's coordinate system for the head</note>, either for more precise investigation or to save effort. It is also permitted to bend the straight lines as needed, however one wishes. And so that I may be more clearly understood, I will hereafter sketch several of the heads described above with their inverted, vertical, transverse, diagonal, and broken lines—among them some curved ones—and then draw the shape of the face back into them. This is for the benefit of those who might not understand this writing on sketching: all the previously described faces, once they have been altered, may be transferred from one to another in many ways and mixed all together, from the last through to the first as one wishes, and handled as strangely as can be imagined. For all things are constantly subject to change. And this should not be forgotten: whatever is taken from the thickness of a face in side view must be added to its width in the front view, and vice versa. Take note of one more instruction: I will shift all vertical and transverse lines in the <term>squaring</term> <note>German: *vierung*—the grid or "quadrature" used to contain the drawing of the head</note> of the side-profile face quite arbitrarily and willfully, and then draw the contour lines of the face back into it. Whatever results from that will be seen here at the end in its figure; every person may do this differently as he pleases.

But such aforementioned opinions serve more for the sake of creating variety than for the shape of beauty; yet one must know such things and the like, so that one may learn many things from much experience. For no one will truly know what produces a good figure unless he first knows what produces a deformity.

<summary>Dürer describes the vast variations in human facial features and explains how his geometric grid system (using vertical, horizontal, and diagonal lines) can be manipulated to draft these differences. He concludes that understanding "deformity" or variation is essential for an artist to eventually grasp true beauty.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, facial features, proportions, geometry in art, grid system, anatomy, artistic theory, deformity vs. beauty</keywords>

<meta>This page contains no handwritten or printed text; it is comprised entirely of woodcut illustrations from Albrecht Dürer’s treatise on human anatomy. The following description serves as a guide to the visual content of this folio.</meta>

->**Studies in Facial Proportions**<-

This page features six masterfully executed <term>woodcuts (Holzschnitt)</term>—a form of printmaking where text and images are carved into a block of wood. These diagrams illustrate the variations of human facial proportions in <term>profile</term> (the side view of the head). 

Each head is contained within a square frame and is meticulously mapped using a series of horizontal and diagonal construction lines. These lines allow the artist to precisely locate and compare features such as the brow, the bridge of the nose, the ear, and the chin. 

These studies are a central part of Dürer's <term>Theory of Proportions (Proportionslehre)</term>. By adjusting the geometric grid, the artist demonstrates how different "types" of people can be systematically drawn. This intersection of <term>anatomy</term> (the study of the body's structure) and mathematics was a hallmark of the Northern Renaissance. 

<note>These illustrations are likely from Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion," published posthumously in 1528.</note>

Beyond mere measurement, these drawings also touch upon <term>physiognomy (Physiognomie)</term>, a historical practice where an individual's character was judged based on their outward facial features. By showing a range of "ideal" and "divergent" facial structures, Dürer provided a toolkit for artists to depict the vast diversity of the human race with mathematical accuracy.

**Furthermore**, I wish to demonstrate how the head may be altered and transformed in its forward-facing aspect. First, one may draw the most varied transverse <term>zwerchlinien|horizontal or cross-lines</term> by transferring them from the various transformed side-profiles into the square of the front-facing head.

Therefore, I will not speak much further regarding the transverse lines in the front-facing head, except to note that one may draw them diagonally <note>original: "vber ort," meaning across or at an angle</note>—some or all of them as one desires—for by such means, crooked faces are produced, just as when one side is made lower than the other. Because of this, I intend only to deal with the upright <term>auffrechte linien|vertical lines</term>.

Now observe that between the two sides of the square <note>Dürer uses the term "Vierung" (squaring) to describe the geometric bounding box or grid used to plot the features.</note> of the face, **a.** and **b.**, there are other upright lines, such as **i. g. c. d. e. f. h. k.**, which encompass and distinguish the most important features in the face according to their width: such as the forehead, eyes, nose, ears, mouth, chin, and the other parts.

It is to be noted that each of these mentioned upright lines in the grid may be shifted individually, or all of them together. Wherever you shift two lines far apart from one another, the features in between will become wide; but at the ends where they run close together, they will become

He notes that while horizontal lines can create "crooked" faces, he focuses here on how vertical adjustments affect the width of facial features like the eyes and nose.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, facial transformation, geometry in art, grid system, Renaissance anatomy, vertical lines, facial width</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical discourse on the "variation" of human features from his 1528 treatise. The previous pages likely established the geometric "grid" or base proportions which he is now teaching the reader to manipulate to create diverse facial types.</meta>

The parts between them may be narrow, whether at the top or the bottom. The straight line may also be drawn in the manner of an arc. Through such aforementioned methods, no line remains upright, but rather leans outward or inward. From such a shifting of the aforementioned lines, so that the shape of the face is drawn anew with these lines, you will find that there are broad or narrow foreheads; likewise, horizontally long or short eyes, set close together or far apart. One may make one larger than the other, with larger or smaller eyelids, and set one higher than the other; the same applies to the eyebrows. There will also be broad or narrow noses, whether they be hooked or straight at the top or bottom, bulbous or pointed, angular or round, broad at the back by the nostrils and narrow at the front, and conversely, large wide nostrils or small and narrow ones. Likewise, broad or narrow mouths, chins, and cheeks; thus some become broad at the top and narrow at the bottom, and vice-versa. 

Through such unequal divisions, crooked, distorted faces and other rarities are created, such as those with narrow or wide maws, crooked, thick, large or small lips <note>original: 'lehsen'; usually referring to thick or protruding lips</note>, or unevenly broad or narrow jawbones, pointed or blunt, whole or cleft. Such and similar things, which belong to so many varieties of distinction, a diligent observer may investigate to their foundation, and through the aforementioned method find many wonders that lie buried herein. And this is a particular truth: he who understands and recognizes what makes a thing ugly and clumsy <note>Dürer uses 'vngeschickt' to mean both physically unskillful and aesthetically inappropriate</note> can thereby perceive what he ought to avoid; and the more that deformity is stripped away, the more the lovely beauty remains. And whoever learns to understand these described things will see in every single thing how the <term>variation</term> <note>original: 'verkeerung'; Dürer's term for the systematic distortion or transformation of base proportions to create different characters</note> is fashioned. This is of great use to anyone who wishes to create a recognizable likeness of a person, whether it be in a painting or in raised figures <note>original: 'erhabnen bilden'; referring to sculpture or reliefs</note>. But whoever is in such a craft and does not understand these things, it is certainly not possible for him to make his work correctly, unless it happens for him by mere chance.

Also, on a long thin neck the head is different than on a thick, short neck. 
<margin>•</margin> Note also that some people have very large features <note>original: 'gebrech'; literally 'faults' or 'breaks', but here referring to prominent, character-defining facial traits</note> in their faces—that is, where the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin fill out significantly. Likewise, some have small features of these aforementioned things.

Also, every head that is made can be altered and made unrecognizable with hair—whether shorn, curly or straight, thick or thin, long or short, in locks or combed, dry or wet. The same applies to the beard or being without a beard; all things used just as with the hair.

From these described methods, I wish to draw out a few faces. Every woman’s head is to be varied just like the man’s, provided that the womanly nature remains. With such variation as mentioned above, one may run through the whole body and search through all the limbs.

And yet, when I speak of what is womanly or manly nature, it is to be understood thus: that one does not change a man or a woman so much that the nature in the man is no longer recognizable as manly, nor in the woman as womanly, though distinctions may be made in size. An example of this is to be taken: that all humans or other animals are like one another within their genus, yet one recognizes the majority among them as female or male. Thus, woman and man must remain human in all things within their distinctions. Among the animals, one sees that no lion is ever so deformed that one would take him for a donkey, nor is a fox seen as a wolf; therefore, no genus of creature should be turned away from its nature according to its order.

<summary>Dürer explains how to systematically vary facial features to create diversity and character in art, while emphasizing that the artist must always preserve the essential "nature" and gender of the subject. He argues that understanding deformity is the key to achieving beauty and avoiding accidental results.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, facial features, physiognomy, artistic theory, gender characteristics, anatomy, Dürer, Renaissance art, aesthetics, variation</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
But when people sometimes say a person looks lion-like <note>original: "lebisch," an archaic term for lion-like</note>, or like a bear, wolf, fox, or dog—even though he does not have four feet like those animals—it does not follow from this that the limbs of the animal are actually present. Rather, it is the human's outward appearance that suggests to us the temperament <note>original: "gemüt," referring to the inner character or disposition</note> of such an animal within a person, according to our own perception. Such comparisons that we create in our minds do not affect the physical limbs; therefore, the nature of one is not mixed into the other.

Therefore, the comparison of differences I am speaking of can partly be attributed to the canine race <note>Dürer uses the vast diversity of dog breeds to illustrate how a single species can have widely different proportions while remaining the same type of creature</note>. For the diversity among them is very great; some are quite large, others very small. They are more than a thousandfold different in their proportions across all their limbs and are of entirely different shapes. They have smooth hair or are quite shaggy, and come in many different colors. And they are more distinct from one another in their form than a wolf is from a fox; yet one always sees that they are neither wolves nor foxes.

From this it is clear that the transformation <note>or "distortion" of proportions through Dürer’s geometric grid system</note> of a form does not change its nature into another; for one can easily distinguish a dog from a wild beast. This itself is a specific distinction of form about which much could be said, but we shall leave it here for the sake of brevity. Therefore, one type should not become another within its species if they are meant to remain distinct. This is said so that you may transform <note>distort or modify using geometric principles</note> the man, yet so that he does not become a woman or something else; and likewise with the woman.

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying the proportions of human figures, following previous discussions on basic measurements. The text is from Albrecht Dürer’s foundational work on human proportion.</meta>

Next, however, I want to show another way by which one may—using the words "thick," "thin," "broad," or "narrow"—measure any previously described figure anew and make it different than it was before: thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower. And a thing is changed through this just as was described before using the words "long" and "short," only through a different application. This must be understood as follows: if the figure made previously is to remain at its first described length, but is to become thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower on the cross-lines <note>The horizontal lines of a measurement grid.</note> and between them. In this case, the line of the figure’s length is already measured. But for the thickness and breadth, you must have another line with which you wish to measure. To enlarge the figure, make this line longer; but if you wish to have the figure more slender <note>original: "geschmeydiger"; literally "supple," here meaning more graceful or lean.</note>, make the other line shorter. And divide all necessary parts through the <term>transferrer</term> <note>original: "verkérer"; a proportional device or scale used to convert measurements from one scale to another.</note> proportionally back into your new line, and then measure the figure with it. Thus, from the longer line, it will be measured thicker and broader, but from the short line, thinner and narrower. For this purpose, I will draw for you below a special figure of two lines; I will call it the <term>twin</term> <note>original: "zwiling"; a double-scale or comparative diagram.</note>, because one uses two types of lines for one thing.

First, I set upright on one side the first divided line of the figure's length, marked 'a' at the top and 'b' at the bottom, which has been divided into its parts as before. And at the bottom end 'b', I join to it at one point a cross-line, shorter or longer according to my pleasure; let the end be 'c'. Thus a <term>triangle</term> <note>original: "triangel"</note> a-b-c is formed. Now, if this cross-line is divided again—either through the divider described in the first booklet or through the <term>transferrer</term>—according to the divisions within the upright line itself, then the <term>twin</term> is ready. Then you may measure your figure with it, and take all your parts from the cross-line regarding whatever concerns "thick," "thin," "broad," or "narrow."

This <term>twin</term> may also be used for the terms "long" and "short." Thus, first, if the upright line a-b is lengthened and divided again into itself through the <term>transferrer</term>, and the figure is then measured with it, the figure will become long and smaller in body according to its proportions. Therefore, whichever line is changed—the upright or the cross-line—and the previously described figure is measured with it, it will always become different. Thus you understand: if the figure you wish to change is to remain at its original length, but is to become larger or smaller in body than it was before, then you must use the transferred cross-line from the <term>twin</term>.

If you wish, however, to let the figure remain at its previously described thickness and breadth, and only make it longer or shorter, you may use the upright line as stated above. Thus you are informed that each of the two sides of the <term>twin</term> is a separate divider, just as was described in the first booklet. Here, one may also use the words "much" or "little."

It is particularly to be noted that through these two types of lines, as "long" and "short," wonderful things are to be measured; and even more wonderful if one of them is moved or shifted from point to point during the measuring; and most wonderful of all if both are shifted or moved. Some builders <note>original: "bawleut"; referring to architects, masons, or engineers who use geometry.</note> will well understand what I say. From these things, matters considered wonderfully difficult are easily discovered by whoever diligently seeks through these methods. Therefore, I have drawn this figure of the <term>twin</term> here below opposite this text, so that you may follow it.
