Dürer highlights how adjusting horizontal and vertical lines independently allows for complex variations in human forms.</summary>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical instructions on adjusting the proportions of the human figure, specifically transitioning from the "thick, strong man" described in his first book to variations that are thinner or wider using his geometric "twin" method.</meta>

# R

**U**sing the "twin" <term>twin (zwiling)</term> <note>A geometric method Dürer used to derive two different body types (one thin, one thick) from a single proportional scheme.</note> described above, I will now describe the first variation of the first thick, strong man from the first book. I will take this figure and, through the "twin" method, alter it—making it thinner, narrower, thicker, and wider than it was previously described.

It should be noted that if, through the aforementioned alterations, a human figure becomes too distorted and loses its natural character—such as when certain parts become far too thin and narrow or too thick and wide—then one must correct these parts using the principles of "giving and taking" <note>Dürer’s mathematical system for compensating for distortion by adding or subtracting units of measurement.</note> which were described earlier.

Therefore, I will hereafter change certain things in the versions that have become thinner and thicker. Namely, in the thinner version, the head should be made shorter; from the highest point of the crown to the end of the chin shall be two 15th-parts <note>Dürer divides the total body height into fractional parts to maintain consistent proportions.</note>. The overall height of the figure shall not be reduced from the top; instead, the face should be shifted upwards. Its division along its length shall remain divided exactly as before. However, the shoulders should be drawn down to the armpits from the line of the chin on both sides. And since the foot became shorter during the "squaring" <term>squaring (vierung)</term> <note>The process of transferring a drawing from one set of coordinates to another, which often caused foreshortening or distortion.</note>, it must be lengthened again so that it measures one 13th-part and one 14th-part. After that, make the legs a bit thinner and narrower according to your judgment, from the line of the buttocks down to the lower ankle.

However, in the enlarged <note>Thicker or more robust.</note> version, I have left the proportions as I described them in the first little book, but the jowl <note>original: "trollen," referring to a roll of flesh or a double chin.</note> under the chin is made larger. You may also treat the head of this version as I did previously with the man’s head. I also make the shoulders higher, from the armpits up toward the neck, and I move the line of the back further down; otherwise, the buttocks would be too tucked in. I also make the leg, when viewed from the side, a little narrower below the knee, and make the foot one 6th-part long. For the legs seen from the front, I make the area around the ankles and the foot a little narrower. I have plotted <note>original: "auffgerissen," meaning to draw or draft geometrically.</note> and placed before your eyes both the smaller and larger versions below.

<summary>Dürer explains how to mathematically adjust the "thick man" archetype into thinner or wider variations while correcting for the distortions caused by geometric projection.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, geometry, squaring, twin method, artistic theory</keywords>

### The Measurement of the Female Figure

*The illustrations on this page demonstrate the mathematical construction of the female body in both profile and frontal views.*

<note>The paper shows significant "bleed-through" or ghosting from the reverse side (the verso). Because the paper used in the 16th century was sometimes thin or the ink particularly heavy, text from the back page is visible here in reverse. The visible reversed words include:</note>

*   **Head** <note>original: "haupt"</note>

The markings on the figures correspond to a complex system of fractions based on the total height of the body.</note>

# The Proportions of the Stout Man

<note>This page features two woodcut illustrations from Albrecht Dürer's 1528 treatise, *Vier Bücher von menschlicher Proportion* (Four Books on Human Proportion). Dürer was the first to apply the rigorous geometry of the Renaissance to the diversity of the human form.</note>

This section illustrates the mathematical proportions of a **stout human figure**, characterized by a broader frame and thicker limbs than the preceding examples. 

<margin>The Strong Man</margin>

The illustration consists of two detailed woodcuts:

*   **On the left:** The figure is shown in **profile** (side view), emphasizing the depth of the torso and the curvature of the spine.
*   **On the right:** The figure is shown from a **frontal view**, demonstrating the width of the shoulders, chest, and hips.

<note>original: "starker Mann"</note>

Dürer uses a <term>system of fractions<gloss>A method where the total body height is divided into units (like the head or foot) to determine the size of every other body part</gloss></term> to ensure the artist can recreate this specific body type accurately. 

<margin>Mathematical Measurements</margin>

The lines crossing the figures correspond to specific anatomical markers—such as the chin, the nipples, the navel, and the knees—which Dürer meticulously calculated to define the "stout" type.

<note>Dürer’s work was revolutionary because it moved away from a single "ideal" beauty (like that of the Greeks) and instead used mathematics to catalog all types of human bodies, from the very thin to the very thick.</note>

<summary>This page presents woodcut studies of a stout male figure in profile and frontal views, using Albrecht Dürer’s mathematical system to define the proportions of a robust physique.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, woodcut, anatomy, Renaissance art, stout figure, male proportions, mathematical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>The text is from a treatise on human proportion, likely by Albrecht Dürer. The preceding page would have discussed the standard proportions for a male figure, whereas this page focuses on "thinning" those proportions to create a more slender aesthetic.</meta>

Further I will describe the fourth man in the first book, by changing him through the <term>proportional divider</term> <note>original: "zwisling" or "zwiling," a geometric tool or scale used to adjust measurements proportionally while maintaining their relationship to one another</note> to make him thinner and narrower than he was before. To do this, I proceed as follows: I make the <term>horizontal line</term> <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> on the divider a sixth part shorter than the vertical line of the man’s total length. I then use the <term>transferrer</term> <note>original: "verkerer," a geometric device or method for converting scales</note> to create a new scale as previously mentioned. However, the vertical line will remain divided by its horizontal lines according to the length as before, as it was described for the man. Only the horizontal line of the <term>armpits</term> <note>original: "ochsen," an archaic term used in Renaissance anatomy to denote the armpit area</note> I move further upward as appropriate, so that the shoulders do not become too thick. I also omit the lower chest line and place the nipples on the previous armpit line, so that they sit higher than before. Additionally, I move the two horizontal lines above the foot further down, so that the <term>instep</term> <note>original: "rist"</note> of the foot does not become too high. After that, using the shorter horizontal line of the divider, I determine all his horizontal lines and the thicknesses and widths in between; thus, a long, thin, and narrow figure is produced.

Likewise, if one wishes to make the head of this figure one-tenth of the total body length, he may do so, provided that the forehead line is moved up to the highest point and the horizontal lines of the face are divided equally again. However, the figure will become long-necked if the head is shifted upward; and make the foot one-seventh part of the length.

To understand all this better, I have <term>sketched</term> <note>original: "auffgerißen," referring to a technical or architectural-style drawing</note> this aforementioned altered man below, along with his corresponding woman—altered just as the man was—so that you may follow these directions.

In the same manner that I moved the horizontal lines for the man and omitted one, I do the same for the woman.

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for altering the proportions of a standard male and female figure to make them appear thinner and taller using a proportional divider. It details specific adjustments to the armpits, chest, and feet to maintain anatomical harmony in the elongated form.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry, Renaissance art, anatomy, drawing techniques, Albrecht Dürer, proportional divider</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on human proportion, likely from Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion." The text transitions from basic measurements to a method for mathematical distortion or "artificial" transformation of a figure using a geometric device.</meta>

## In what follows, I will show yet another way that one might artificially <note>original: "felschlich." In this context, it refers to a mathematical transformation or deliberate distortion for artistic effect rather than "falsification" in the sense of a forgery.</note> change a previously described figure—and yet do so in an orderly way—so that every part previously made is enhanced through this method. This is done if one wishes to elongate all the parts upward and shorten them downward. Or, one can do the opposite: shorten the parts of the figure upward and elongate them downward. In all of this, the thickness and width of the figure remain the same on all horizontal lines <term>horizontal lines; original: "zwerch linien"</term> and in all the proportions <term>proportions or limbs; original: "glidmaß"</term> between them, just as they were before. Or, if one wishes, one could make the figure thinner and narrower where it is elongated at the top, as the situation requires. Likewise, in the opposite figure—where the upper parts are shortened and the lower parts elongated—the horizontal lines and what lies between them may be thickened and widened.

But how this is to be done, I will now show. I place a straight horizontal line **a. b.** across; beneath it, I place a point **c.** If you wish to greatly expand the parts at the top, place point **c.** closer to the horizontal line **a. b.** But if you do not want to expand the parts excessively, place point **c.** well below the horizontal line **a. b.** Now observe and keep in mind: let line **a. b.** be the base line <note>original: "soln lini." Likely referring to the "sole" of the foot or the ground line of the drawing.</note> of the figure. Above this base line **a. b.**, I draw another horizontal parallel line <term>parallel line; original: "barlini"</term> **f. g.** as high as the length of the figure I am describing, and between them, I place an upright dotted line in which the limbs are divided according to length; let the top be **h.** and the bottom **i.** Thereafter, I draw a horizontal line **k. l.** exactly in the middle between **f. g.** and **a. b.** 

Once that is done, I draw two lines from point **c.** upward as long as I need them; let their ends be **d. e.** These split apart from each other at the top and must be movable <note>In the physical construction of such a drafting aid, these lines might be represented by threads or a pivoting ruler used to calculate proportions.</note>; with them, one must measure the parts that are to become larger and smaller. However, both lines must remain fixed together at point **c.** below. 

Now, if you take a part that you wish to make larger or smaller—whether it be at the top or the bottom—and want to find how it should rightly be done, do this: regardless of which horizontal line the part to be measured stands upon, always take the same width or thickness as it was described in the first figure, and place it in the center on the horizontal line **k. l.**, so that the upright line **h. i.** passes through the middle of the part placed upon it. Then, if you let the two movable lines **d. e.** touch this part on both sides, they will give you the increased width above on the horizontal line where it should stand. In this way, this method increases and diminishes everything above and below the line **k. l.**, upward and downward. All of this may also be turned upside down and used in reverse; then everything will be the opposite of the first way.

I have drawn this figure twice in what follows—the second time reversed—and I give it a special name, calling it the **Indicator** <term>Indicator; original: "Zeyger." A specialized drafting tool or geometric diagram described by Dürer for scaling proportions.</term>.

->*A*<-

The text continues the technical instructions for geometric distortion from the previous section. The handwriting and print quality are consistent with a 16th-century treatise on proportion, likely by Albrecht Dürer.

# But so that I return to the distortion (for which this above-described indicator is especially to be used and will be better understood), I will say how one should make it. Do it thus: Take for yourself the fifth described man and his corresponding woman from the second booklet, and as their upright lines of their length are divided by the horizontal lines. Let one of these same upright marked lines be **a.** at the top and **b.** at the bottom; set this upright before you. From point **b.**, extend a horizontal line at right angles; let its end be **c.** After that, draw a diagonal line from **a.** to **c.** Then, from the marked line of the figure, draw straight lines from all the points that indicate the length of the divisions into point **c.** Thus this tool is ready, from which you must take the distortion of the figure that is lengthened in its parts at the top, and shall be prepared for this purpose; likewise for the opposite effect. Therefore, this tool shall henceforth be called the "below-shortener," or more specifically the <term>distorter</term> <note>original: "felscher"; here referring to a geometric tool for altering proportions</note>, because anything measured in it with a hanging line falls incorrectly.

Now you must first learn its use, thus: Take another line of the figure’s length, let it be **d.** at the top and **e.** at the bottom. Place this line with its lower end **e.** on the line **b. c.** and push it back from the angle **b.** toward the point **c.** about an eighth of the figure's length. Lean its top end **d.** against the diagonal line **a. c.** Then this line **d. e.** will be intersected and proportionately divided by the lines drawn from the points of the figure’s division into point **c.**, and yet it is distorted. For everything lengthens toward the top and becomes shorter toward the bottom. If you now place this newly created marked line before you—which is **d.** at the top and **e.** at the bottom—and draw horizontal lines through all the points, and upon these and between them create the previously described thicknesses and widths of the figure, and draw the shape of the figure into it anew, you will then see its transformation. For this reason, I have drawn this <term>distorter</term> below twice—and on the second one, the bottom is turned to the top to correctly direct oneself. And for this reason: if you wish to reverse the figure to the opposite of the aforementioned, then turn your marked line upside down and use it according to the previously described meaning. And of such two figures that have been distorted through the above-mentioned means, I have drawn a pair—a man and a woman—below. However, I have neither enlarged nor diminished them at the top or bottom, as was spoken of above in the indicator; but whoever wishes to may use it so. And whoever considers this <term>distorter</term> well and uses it correctly in many kinds of things will achieve and make wonderful things and create much use. For the previous words of distinction are all to be considered herein, in order to find hidden things necessary for such work. But do not give or take as much from things as is done here. Anyone who wishes may, using the <term>scale</term> <note>original: "maßstab"; a reference tool for consistent measurement</note>, describe anew all these previously made and altered figures and how they came to be.

However, if someone wishes to describe them, let him flee the deformity that arises from too much distortion. I do not present these things as if they must be thus and not otherwise; rather, I have set them so far out of their true nature as a lesson, so that from such gross distinctions one may clearly recognize that all these previously described things should be used out of necessity and not for destruction. Therefore, do not push the leaning line in the <term>distorter</term> too far back on the horizontal line, whether it be upright or inverted, and guard yourself so that your transformations do not become <term>monsters</term> <note>original: "wechselbelg"; literally "changelings," implying deformed or unnatural creatures</note>. For as useful as these things are when used out of necessity, they are just as misleading when used without need or out of <term>frivolity</term> <note>original: "bierwitz"; refers to a sort of foolish over-cleverness or misplaced ingenuity</note>, in excess, when one does too much without cause. This <term>distorter</term> may be used in specific parts just as well as in the whole. From such various transformations of the human form, one finds which are stronger or weaker, nimbler or slower, free or heavy. Therefore, it is necessary to know all this, for such things extend much further than is indicated here.

<summary>This page explains how to construct and use a geometric "distorter" tool to alter human proportions for artistic effect, allowing for the depiction of different physical temperaments. The author cautions the artist to use these distortions sparingly to avoid creating unnatural or monstrous figures.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry, distortion, Albrecht Dürer, artistic theory, foreshortening, physical types, anatomy</keywords>

<meta>This page appears to be from a technical manual on human proportion, likely by Albrecht Dürer. It provides instructions on how to use specific geometric tools to manipulate and then correct the proportions of male and female figures.</meta>

Furthermore, if someone wishes to use the pair of figures—man and woman—that have been altered by the **distorter** <note>original: *felscher*, a geometric device or grid used to intentionally compress or stretch proportions for artistic effect</note> for the sake of novelty, they should do the following: for the two figures that have been elongated at the top, the head above the forehead must be made significantly lower, and the insteps of the feet should be made higher. 

However, for the other pair of figures that were elongated at the bottom, the shins and the insteps of the feet should be made much shorter; then, one should redistribute all the parts equally again using the **converter** <note>original: *verkerer*, a secondary tool or mathematical process used to re-proportion distorted measurements back into a harmonious scale</note>. But in the figures that follow, I have left everything exactly as they turned out.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from Albrecht Dürer's "Manual of Measurement" (1525), specifically focusing on geometric transformations. The previous section likely discussed the first type of distortion tool; here, Dürer introduces a second method intended for projecting images onto curved surfaces like vaults.</meta>

I will now draw and show you another **distorter** <note>original: "felſcher" — Dürer's term for a transformation grid or geometric device used to intentionally distort or "falsify" proportions for artistic effect.</note> so that a previously described human figure may be transformed in length. However, in its thickness and width, it is transformed in an orderly way through the **transfer-scale** <note>original: "weier" — likely a reference to a proportional scale or a specific diagrammatic tool Dürer used to maintain consistency between dimensions.</note>; one may use this as often as needed. It can be measured in two ways: one measures outwardly on the arc of a **compass** <note>original: "zirckels" — here referring to the curved line or arc created by a compass.</note>, the other inwardly on the arc of a hollow circle. Each person can find what to do with this for as long as it pleases them. Whoever understands this with the right mind will surely realize what it should be used for. Through this, one can also understand why a straight, parallel ruler appears curved in a round mirror; if it stands upright in front of the middle of the mirror, it gains a bulging belly in the front and becomes wide, but narrow at the top and bottom and bent backwards. Likewise, all other things align themselves according to the circle. Therefore, for anyone who wishes to paint on **vaults** <note>original: "gwelber" — curved or arched ceilings where perspective distortion is necessary to make figures look "correct" from the floor.</note>, it is necessary to know such things. Although this is not yet **perspective** <note>original: "perspectiua" — Dürer notes that while these are geometric transformations, they differ from the formal rules of linear perspective.</note>, it follows along when one sees such things in a mirror. But here I speak only of measurement itself, for this distorter only shows what is measured on a round or hollow object from a straight one. But the reason why it happens this way you will realize and see in the transformation of the images when you draw them upon this distorter, for they [the reasons] reveal themselves. And although it looks strange in the drawing where it is not helped by practical use and seems useless, it is nonetheless necessary to know this and all other practices so that an expert may extract what is useful to him; however, the unlearned may mock this and even greater things.

Now I will show how to set up this other **distorter** so that a previously made image may be transformed from it; and if this is used correctly, it brings improvement to certain things, so avoid talking too much about it. But the reason I do so much with it here is so that the differences may be more noticeably recognized in a small version, and also because I do not have space here to make it large; therefore, use this thing moderately in larger works. Construct this distorter thus: First, take the length of the image on its line, which is divided with all its points. Stand it upright before you and mark its ends **x.** at the top and **y.** at the bottom. After that, divide **x.y.** in the middle from each other with a point **c.** From this point, draw a straight line at equal angles <note>at right angles</note> across; let the end be **z.** After that, draw two lines **x.z.** and **y.z.**; thus a long **triangle** <note>original: "triangel"</note> is formed, and **z.** is the tip. After that, draw lines from all the points of the upright line **x.y.** into the point **z.** as far as you need them. After that, draw an upright **parallel line** <note>original: "bar lini" — from the Latin "par-linea."</note> behind the line **x.y.** toward the point **c.** as long as you need it, so that it goes far above and below the front line of the triangle. After that, take a long **compass** <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and set one foot in the point **z.** and with the other foot set it on the middle line of the triangle near the upright **x.y.** so that it touches the line **x.y.** at one point. From there, draw a round circle arc upwards and downwards until it reaches the back-set upright parallel line. And where this round stroke cuts the back-set upright parallel line, place an **s.** at the top and a **t.** at the bottom. And where this round stroke subsequently cuts the lines that were drawn from the points of line **x.y.** to point **z.**, draw straight horizontal parallel lines from all those same points into the back-set upright line **s.t.** In this way, the line **s.t.** is divided from the line **x.y.**, but it changes entirely: first, because it becomes shorter; and second, because it is drawn from the points of the circle, and thus all things shorten themselves upward and downward the longer [further from the center] they are. After that, mark all the points in this newly formed line above the middle. Mark the topmost cross-line with an **a.**, the next **b.**, then **c.**, and so on: **d.e.f.g.** etc. But below the middle, use numbers; start at the very bottom with a **1.**, then **2. 3. 4. 5.** etc. These...

<summary>Dürer explains a geometric method for distorting images using a triangular grid and compass arcs, specifically intended to help artists paint correctly on curved surfaces like vaults. He provides step-by-step instructions for constructing a "distorter" (transformation grid) that compresses proportions as they move away from a central viewing point.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, geometry, transformation grid, vaults, anamorphism, perspective, compass construction, proportional scaling</keywords>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geometric figure construction, following the signature S iij. The catchword "so" from the previous page leads into the instructions for using proportional lines.</meta>

<sig>S iiij</sig>

**Line *a. i.*** with its contents and the full length of the <term>proportions</term><note>The original word is "glidmaß," referring to the proportional measurements of the human body or its limbs.</note> should be used as described before. Place two of these lines at the same height next to each other: the first for the side-view figure and the second for the front-view figure. Draw horizontal lines through all your newly created divisions. Once the lengths of the parts are divided, you must then find how to divide the thickness and the width. Do this using the <term>transferrer</term><note>Original: "weiler." A geometric instrument or proportional compass used to transfer measurements from one scale to another.</note> with its movable line. You must use it on both sides so that it gives you equal measure at the top and bottom.

First, draw a straight horizontal line where the two ends are marked ***a.*** and ***a.*** In the middle, set an upright vertical line at right angles; let this be ***g.*** at the top and ***b.*** at the bottom. Now, make two movable lines ***a. c.***, one on each side. You must use them as follows: In the <term>distorter</term><note>Original: "felscher." A geometric device or diagram designed by Dürer to "falsify" or transform standard proportions into new variations (e.g., making a figure thinner, wider, or more tapered).</note> you just made, take the greatest width between the straight line and the arc on the middle horizontal line of the triangle (which is drawn from point ***i.*** through the arc ***f. t.***). Place one end of this measurement upright on the vertical line ***g. b.*** starting from the horizontal line of the <term>transferrer</term> ***a. a.*** At this height, place the two movable lines ***a. c.*** so that they cross over each other.

Afterward, take the upper half of the horizontal lines between the arc and its fixed or straight line for each of its lengths. Note which letters they are marked with and place them upright on one side of the <term>transferrer</term>, so that one end of each touches the horizontal line of the <term>transferrer</term> ***a. a.*** Move them parallel back and forth on the horizontal line ***a. a.*** until their tops all touch the placed line ***a. c.*** In this way, all the short lines in the <term>transferrer</term> move toward point ***a.***, just as they are closest to point ***f.*** in the arc of the <term>distorter</term>. However, the distance between them does not remain as it was in the arc because of the arc's curvature—for the straight line compared to the curved one creates a change. Just as I place the upper half marked with letters from the <term>distorter</term> into one part of the <term>transferrer</term>, I do the same with the other half of the arc. I take the parts from the bottom of the <term>distorter</term> marked with numbers and place them in the other half of the <term>transferrer</term>. 

Once this is prepared, take every thickness or width of the figure described previously on every horizontal line (and those in between) and place them in the <term>transferrer</term> on the vertical line ***g. b.*** so that they touch the horizontal line ***a. a.*** at the bottom. Then, place your movable line ***a. c.*** upon it; this will cut through all the vertical lines that stand in the <term>transferrer</term>. Therefore, whichever vertical line a width or thickness is taken from, its correct measurement will be found through this placing and cutting—just as I have very precisely drawn hereafter. Thus, I have also drawn below two figure measurements and methods, which I described at the end of the other booklet, transformed and drawn through this <term>distorter</term>. Only the arms, hands, and feet have I not made with this <term>distorter</term>, but rather by my own skill, because this <term>distorter</term> leads one too far astray when it reaches the highest or lowest points.

Also, if one reverses this <term>distorter</term> and uses it in the opposite way to transform a figure, that same figure will be elongated more and more in its upper and lower parts, but shortened more and more toward the middle, unlike the previously transformed figure. To make this inverted <term>distorter</term>, do the following: Take again the dotted line ***r. y.*** of the figure’s length, set it upright before you, and extend it with ***f. t.*** as before. Then, set your point ***z.*** and draw the two lines ***r. z.*** and ***z. y.*** Afterward, draw straight lines from all the points on line ***r. y.*** to point ***z.*** Then, set a point ***t.*** across from ***z.*** at the same height. Take a long <term>compass</term><note>Original: "zirckel."</note>, set one foot on point ***t.*** and the other on point ***s.***, and draw a circle around point ***t.*** so that the arc is between ***r. y.*** and point ***z.*** Where this compass mark cuts the straight lines drawn from the points between ***r. y.*** to point ***z.***, draw horizontal <term>parallel lines</term><note>Original: "barliniē."</note> from those intersections. Behind the arc, toward point ***z.***, draw a vertical line through all those horizontal lines. This is then the newly found line, newly divided with horizontal lines through the entire length of the figure. After this, you must seek the thickness and width of the figure through the <term>transferrer</term> as before, but inverted. Let whoever wishes to do so draw a figure in this way themselves, for hereafter I have drawn nothing more than this <term>distorter</term> for that purpose.

<summary>This page provides complex geometric instructions for using two instruments, the "distorter" and the "transferrer," to mathematically alter the proportions of human figures. It explains how to map measurements from curved arcs to straight lines to achieve consistent, though intentionally distorted, physical dimensions.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry, Albrecht Dürer, distortion, transferrer, measurement, drafting tools, anatomical drawing</keywords>
